<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/alignment-healthcare-sues-cms-over-its-2027-medicare-advantage-star-ratings" hreflang="en">Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Elevance Health is the latest insurer to sue the feds over its performance in the latest round of star ratings for the Medicare Advantage program.

The company sued the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services in District of Columbia federal court on Friday, arguing that the 2027 star ratings calculations violated the Administrative Procedure Act in using 11 measures that it says were adopted unlawfully.

In the lawsuit, the company said that the harm from including these measures is "concrete and substantial." If they were excluded, it would increase the scores for 12 of its Medicare Advantage contracts and generate $516 million in additional quality bonus payments.

"Their inclusion therefore does more than violate statutory requirements in the abstract: it depresses Elevance Health’s ratings and reduces the funding available for the following payment year," per the lawsuit. "That funding could support broader supplemental benefits, lower premiums and cost-sharing, and improved care coordination."

"CMS’s unlawful methodology puts those resources out of reach," the company said.

In addition to the financial impacts, Elevance argues that the star ratings scores can also lead to reputational damage that makes it harder for them to compete in the MA market. It also provides prospective enrollees with inaccurate information, the company said.

"Beneficiaries cannot make informed coverage choices when the government’s own comparison tool conveys a distorted assessment of the plans available to them," Elevance said.

"An artificially depressed rating does not merely place Elevance Health at a competitive disadvantage; it undermines the very comparison the program is supposed to enable," the company continued. "A rating lowered by unlawful measures presents beneficiaries with a misleading signal of relative quality, rather than a lawful basis for distinguishing among competing plans."

CVS Heath's Aetna is joining the ranks of insurers suing over its results in the 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

The insured filed suit Monday in Georgia's Southern District Court, and argues that 28 measures used by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services to calculate its scores were "unlawfully adopted" and should have been excluded.

The court is the same venue that ruled in favor of Clover Health over the summer, and set aside 20 measures that were used to determine its 2026 scores. That decision, which the feds have appealed, kicked off a broad recalculation of 2026 scores that looked different for insurers outside of Clover, triggering a wave of litigation.

In its lawsuit, Aetna cites the Clover decision in finding that CMS extended beyond its statutory authority in deploying those 20 measures.

The company said that calculating its scores using the 28 measures in question will likely cost it $2 billion in 2028 quality bonus payments and rebate retention. Those are funds that the insurer could "apply toward expanding member benefits, reducing premiums and investing in quality improvement."

Aetna adds that in addition to the monetary loss, having lower scores negatively impacts the company's reputation and its ability to attract potential members.

"The harm to CVS Health is not exclusively financial; lower ratings carry meaningful reputational harm, which can in turn impact membership," per the lawsuit. "By incorporating unlawful measures into the 2027 Star Ratings calculation, CMS cast CVS Health’s ratings in a poorer light in the eyes of Medicare Advantage consumers relative to peer MAOs, undermining CVS Health’s ability to compete for Medicare beneficiaries through enhanced benefits and the like."

Aetna did not respond to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Based on an analysis from Stephens, 69% of Aetna's MA members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 81%. That threshold is the key to securing quality bonus payments.

Aetna's complaint follows others focused on the 2027 scores from Centene and Alignment Healthcare (see those stories below).

Centene has become the latest insurer to sue the federal government over its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

In a lawsuit filed Thursday in Georgia's Southern District Court, the company argues that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services violated the Administrative Procedure Act in determining its latest scores. Centene says that CMS ran afoul of "core statutory safeguards" in deploying 17 measures.

The use of those measures ultimately caused "significant injury" to the company, according to the complaint. For 2027, 9% of Centene's members are enrolled in plans with at least four stars, which declined from 18% in the prior year.

"In implementing each of the individual Star measures that Centene challenges, CMS violated applicable law and thereby caused Centene to lose out on hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue that Centene could use to strengthen benefits, reduce premiums, and improve health outcomes," the company said in the lawsuit.

CMS argues that the court has already sided with a similar argument in its decision in favor of Clover Health earlier this year. In that case, which led to a broad recalculation in scores, the courts set aside 20 measures that were used in determining Clover's star ratings for 2026.

If the 17 measures in question had been excluded in calculating Centene's scores, the company estimates it would receive $448 million more in quality bonus payments for the 2028 plan year, funds it would deploy to expand benefits, lower premiums and enhance programs like care coordination.

In addition to the financial impact, a drop in star ratings also carries a reputational hit, Centene said.

"Beyond the financial impact to the company and its members, depressed ratings also inflict significant reputational damage and diminished membership," the company said in the complaint

Centene had not returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Alignment Healthcare sued federal regulators, alleging that its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings were determined "in flagrant violation" of statute.

In the lawsuit filed Friday in District of Columbia federal court, the insurer says that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services "has completely departed from Congress’s vision of leveraging predictable, valid measures developed with input from providers, plans, and the public" in establishing the methodology for the star ratings.

The company argues that rather than establish "consistent, stable specifications" for metrics through the traditional rule-making process, the agency instead detailed key measures through a series of "technical notes." This violates the Administrative Procedure Act, per the lawsuit.

The program's star ratings are the critical determinant in quality bonus payments to insurers as well as a tool that aims to make it easier for enrollees and brokers to identify benefits with the highest quality.

"Consistent application of the statutory requirements governing Star Ratings is therefore essential not only to plan payments and competition but also to ensuring that beneficiaries can rely on the Star Ratings as meaningful and comparable measures of plan quality," Alignment said in the lawsuit.

The company adds that multiple recent court decisions have set aside CMS' star ratings methodology, notably a ruling over the summer in favor of Clover Health that tossed 20 metrics used in the 2026 calculations. A Georgia federal judge determined that 10 measures utilized exceeded CMS' authority, while 10 other measures were promulgated inappropriately.

CMS recalculated the scores across the board following the ruling, but only set aside the 10 measures that the courts determined were outside the bounds of its regulatory authority. The agency maintained that approach in the 2027 calculations, Alignment argues.

"Congress created the Star Ratings program to allow Medicare Advantage beneficiaries to make informed decisions about plan enrollment based on clinically validated measures of plan quality that had been subjected to notice and comment," the company said. "CMS’s contrary approach fails to carry out the vision that Congress set forth by statute."

The Trump administration has appealed the Clover decision, so that legal case remains ongoing.

In addition, Alignment argues it suffered reputational and financial damage as a result of its 2027 scores, which plummeted compared to the prior year. Just 25% of its members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 100% in 2026.

Shares in the company tumbled following the release of the ratings, though stock prices have rebounded since.

Alignment sued the feds following the Clover decision to secure a recalculation for its scores that aligns with the court's decision, and in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said that the lawsuit aligns with that perspective.

"Our lawsuit seeks to ensure that the Star Ratings program operates as Congress intended and that Alignment is properly compensated for the care we provide," Alignment said in the statement. "We respect the judicial process and will present our case in court. Because this matter involves pending litigation, we will not comment on the specific legal arguments beyond our filings."

"Our focus remains on delivering exceptional care, improving health outcomes and advocating for a stable, lawful regulatory framework that supports seniors and strengthens the Medicare Advantage program," the company said.

The Top 5 Biotech Stocks I'd Buy Right Now Without Any Hesitation

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These are among the best the industry has to offer.

Biotech stocks have performed well in 2026, but things could change fairly rapidly. The industry is notoriously volatile, after all, and there are significant regulatory issues that may weigh on biotech companies. However, regardless of what happens over the next year or two, there are several biotechs with attractive prospects that are worth investing in for the long haul. Let's consider five of them.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX +0.85%) has long been the leader in the cystic fibrosis (CF) medicines market, and the company's core therapeutic area remains a meaningful growth driver, with no major patent cliff expected over the next 10 years. However, the company is also making strides elsewhere. Consider Vertex's efforts in kidney diseases. It is inching closer to an approval for povetacicept, a medicine for IgA nephropathy.

Its Phase 3 pipeline also features inaxaplin, an investigational drug for APOL-1-mediated kidney disease. The biotech boasts several recent approvals whose sales should ramp up over the next few years, and it has also diversified its pipeline. Vertex's dominance in its core market and progress elsewhere make the stock a buy.

While Vertex's growth comes primarily from a single therapeutic area, Amgen (AMGN +0.03%) has taken a different approach. In the third quarter, the company had 17 products that delivered at least double-digit year-over-year sales growth. It also had 17 medicines with at least $1 billion annual revenue run rate. That's impressive, and it helps explain why Amgen has been able to overcome recent major patent cliffs without too much trouble, including the one for denosumab, a medicine for several bone-related conditions.

The drugmaker's deep pipeline, which features MariTide -- a highly promising weight-loss medicine -- should enable it to extend its approved lineup. Finally, Amgen is also an excellent dividend stock, having increased its payouts every year since initiating them in 2011.

Gilead Sciences' (GILD -0.41%) portfolio of medicines features some of the best-selling HIV drugs, such as Biktarvy and Descovy for PrEP. The company still generates the bulk of its sales from this area, and one recent approval has helped strengthen the company's position. Gilead Sciences earned approval for Bixlenvo, an HIV pill. However, Gilead Sciences has also made headway in other areas, particularly oncology, in recent years.

Gilead Sciences is betting big on its cancer drug pipeline, which is larger than its HIV pipeline. And as it launches new products in this area, the company will have a stronger, more diversified lineup. Meanwhile, revenue and earnings growth remain strong for the company, and the dividend is rock solid, too. Gilead Sciences has increased its payouts by 74.5% over the past decade.

Exelixis (EXEL +1.25%) isn't the most popular biotech company. It is much smaller than all of the others discussed here. However, Exelixis has managed to become successful in a corner of the vast and highly competitive oncology market. The company's most important medicine, Cabometyx, has earned multiple indications across multiple cancer types.

Cabometyx will likely face generic competition in the U.S. by early 2030, but Exelixis is ready for it. The company could earn approval for zanzalintinib, an investigational medicine for colorectal cancer, next year. It will target additional indications while also developing new cancer medicines. Exelixis' position in oncology makes it an attractive biotech to buy.

Regeneron Pharmaceuticals (REGN +0.16%) has encountered stiff competition for Eylea, a medicine that treats several eye-related diseases, in recent years. This has harmed the company's financial results. However, the drugmaker is slowly putting this issue in the rearview mirror. Revenue and earnings growth have rebounded, largely thanks to a new formulation of Eylea and Dupixent, a medicine for eczema. Regeneron's long-term plans feature both the new version of Eylea and Dupixent.

Dupixent may run into a patent cliff early next decade, but Regeneron will likely seek to protect its empire, notably by developing a high-dose formulation of Dupixent. The company also boasts an attractive pipeline of programs to diversify its lineup, including promising weight-loss candidates. Regeneron's comeback may just be getting started. It's still time to buy the stock.

Prosper Junior Bakiny has positions in Exelixis and Vertex Pharmaceuticals. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amgen, Exelixis, Gilead Sciences, Regeneron Pharmaceuticals, and Vertex Pharmaceuticals. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Trump says renewed Iran strikes ‘highly unlikely’ if talks progress but warns he could still strike

📅 2026-11-08  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump said he is "highly unlikely" to restart open war with Iran, but warned he could still strike the country as Washington tries to reach a deal with Tehran to end the conflict.

The now more than eight-month-old war, which stoked inflation and sent oil prices surging, has seen repeated bursts of fighting separated by ceasefires and peace talks for months. It was widely speculated that Trump might restart open conflict after the midterm elections, where his Republican Party lost badly to Democrats.

Trump's comments came during an interview with CNN, where he said restarting the war is unlikely as long as talks to constrain Iran's nuclear program and reopen the Strait of Hormuz make progress.

"It depends on whether or not they sign a very strong agreement," Trump said. "The answer is highly unlikely it's going to be necessary. They're a failing nation."

Trump, however, left the door open for additional strikes if he deems it necessary. The president reiterated his threats to strike bridges and power plants in Iran if the war restarts.

"I've got three aircraft carriers here. The other one just arrived. We have three. They're fully locked and loaded," the president said. "We have 38 bridges in our site that we can knock down in literally one hour."

An end to the war and reopening of the Strait of Hormuz would likely ease oil prices, though finding a truce has proven difficult. All previous attempts to end the war and reopen the strait have been met with disappointment.

Trump is likely to come under increased pressure to end the conflict, however, now that Democrats have won control of both the House and the Senate. Some Republicans are also likely to be eager for an end to the war, given the role high gas prices played in their drubbing at the ballot box.

The president has demanded Iran fully open the Strait of Hormuz and agree to never develop a nuclear weapon as part of any deal.

Later in the day on Truth Social, Trump posted that "Task Force Iron Gate is in full force and effect in the Islamic Republic of Iran."

"Coupled with history's greatest Naval Blockade, very little gets into, or out of, Iran!" he said.

Central Command announced Task Force Iron Gate on Saturday, describing it as an initiative aimed at "preventing the movement of advanced weapons systems, dual-use technologies, sanctioned materials, and illegal revenue streams across Iran's land borders and the Caspian Sea."

The U.S. has maintained a naval blockade of Iranian ports for months.

Here's How Much AAA Says It Costs To Own A Car In 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When we consider whether we can afford a car, so much of the focus tends to be on the sticker price that we often forget that the costs don't stop once we've made our purchase. Small, everyday expenses that come alongside, like fuel, servicing, finance interest, depreciation, insurance, and tax, aren't always obvious, but the true cost of car ownership adds up over time.

With that in mind, the car valuation experts at AAA performed a study to investigate car ownership costs in 2026. It turns out that drivers are facing ongoing price increases, with the AAA 2026 Your Driving Costs study revealing that the annual cost of owning and running a new car has soared to $12,863, or $1,071.92 per month this year. That's up from an average of $11,577 or $964.78 per month in 2025.

At that rate, ownership costs can add 32.67% on top of the $39,376 sales-weighted average MSRP of new vehicles. The larger bills have been linked to surging fuel prices, faster depreciation, and higher finance interest rates. Still, your specific costs can vary greatly, depending on your car's fuel type.

Owning any car at all is getting more expensive, but the AAA study revealed something more fundamental: Electric cars cost more to own than their traditional fuel-powered and hybrid counterparts. That's largely because depreciation on EVs is worse than gas or hybrid cars. On top of that, those who drive electric cars face higher fees and finance costs. Where EVs tend to do better is in fuel expenses, as they typically cost 66% to 70% less to run than gasoline cars.

Looking at a set of average consumption figures, AAA found that regular-grade gasoline costs $4.152 per gallon on average, while electric cars charged at home averaged as little as 18 cents per kilowatt hour. EVs have seen smaller electricity rate increases, too. The AAA data shows electricity prices have jumped 7.8% since 2025, while gas costs have increased a shocking 31.8%.

Even so, owning and running EVs costs more than gas-powered models, due to higher fees, finance costs, and depreciation, with certain EVs said to depreciate about twice as fast as their gas-powered counterparts. That contributed to pushing EV ownership costs above gas-powered models across the medium sedan, compact SUV, and pickup truck segments.

Nowhere is that gap more pronounced than in the medium sedan segment, where gas-powered medium sedans cost $3,080 less to own (at $10,582 per year) than comparable EV medium sedans ($13,662). Hybrids are even cheaper (at $10,204) and cost the least to own in every category, including the medium SUV class, where EVs win back some ground against gas-powered models, costing $14,332 versus $14,431 for gasoline SUVs.

As a car owner, every mile you drive costs money, but it is entirely possible to keep costs down. One way to do this is to shop around for cheaper insurance. Compare quotes from providers, or even consider buying a car from one of the cheapest brands to insure.

If you are team gas-powered cars, getting a model with great fuel efficiency is just as important. It should be easy enough, seeing as gasoline cars are now much more efficient than they used to be, with some of the most fuel-efficient models released over the years rated to return as high as 58 mpg.

You'll also want to consider depreciation. It is one of the single largest ownership costs and can add a weighted average of $4,422 per year to the cost of running some popular models, according to the AAA study. Toyota, Subaru, and Honda are some of the best brands for value retention, but if you're after something more premium, there's always Lexus. It's the luxury car brand with the best resale value, according to CarEdge.

However, maintenance and spare parts are usually more expensive for luxury marques than mainstream brands like Toyota or Honda. Speaking of which, staying on top maintenance is yet another important part of vehicle ownership that can help keep costs low. Not only does it help prevent larger bills down the road, keeping up with car maintenance also provides a strong service history that supports resale value when it comes time to sell.

Why Singapore Airlines Uses Boeing 787-10s On Short Regional Routes

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Boeing built the 787-10 Dreamliner as the largest Dreamliner variant with the shortest range. That makes it well suited to high-frequency, high-demand medium-length routes. Yet, with a maximum range of 7,500 nautical miles (13,890 km), it is still capable of traditional long-haul missions. Singapore Airlines takes its 787-10s in a different direction, using them to fly bizarrely short routes, typically the domain of narrowbody jets.

Singapore Airlines uses its Dreamliner in an unexpected way for several reasons. The 787-10 is a modern jet that replaced the carrier's older widebody aircraft. It has a high-density configuration ideal for ferrying a large number of passengers. It is also fuel-efficient and versatile, making it cost-effective on a range of routes. The answer is also in Singapore Airlines' current fleet, which lacks capacity for short-haul routes with other jets.

Cirium data reveals that Singapore Airlines will fly the 787-10 on 26 routes throughout November. This is for a total of 1,129 operations and over 380,000 seats, with each jet having 337 seats available onboard. The majority of these 26 routes are regional, coming in at less than a third of the 787-10's range.

The table below includes details on the ten shortest 787-10 routes scheduled for November. All of these routes originate at Singapore Changi Airport (SIN):

I Gusti Ngurah Rai International Airport (DPS)

Guangzhou Baiyun International Airport (CAN)

Hazrat Shahjalal International Airport (DAC)

These routes have impressive frequency, showing that Singapore Airlines uses its Dreamliners to move many people over short distances. The fifth-shortest route in November is the carrier's most frequent route that uses the Dreamliner, with 120 monthly frequencies from SIN to MNL. This route is 1,474 miles long and produces almost 60 million available seat miles. That is more than all but two of the routes.

Another exceptionally frequent route included is the third-shortest, taking passengers 876 miles (1,410 km) between SIN and BKK. The airline will complete this route 106 times in November, offering 35,722 seats. This produces over 31 million available seat miles. Meanwhile, it will run daily operations on the shortest route, traveling just 675 miles from SIN to SGN. The over 10,000 seats produce 6.8 million available seat miles.

The 787-10's position within the Singapore Airlines' fleet shows sound logic behind this usage. The carrier lacks narrowbody options that would traditionally operate these short services. Its only single-aisle aircraft are its 26 Boeing 737 MAX 8 jets. These planes have a total capacity of 154 seats split between economy class and business class. This is less than half the 337-passenger capacity of Singapore Airlines' 787-10s. This means the airline's 737 fleet has a total capacity of 4,004 passengers, compared with 9,436 for its Dreamliners. The 787-10 can simply take more of a chunk out of the high-capacity needs within a region packed with huge cities.

The table below shows Singapore Airlines' current fleet, using Planespotters.net data, revealing the position of the 787-10 within it:

On the other hand, all the carrier's widebody aircraft are overengineered for short-haul routes. The most numerous widebody aircraft in its fleet is the A350-900. This jet has a massive range of 8,500 nautical miles (15,750 km), which can extend to 9,700 nautical miles (17,964 km). As a result, the A350-900 has proven effective on long-haul and premium-dense routes. +

The airline often flies its MAX aircraft alongside its larger widebody services to ensure appropriate capacity is deployed on various routes.

Singapore Airlines' Dreamliner configuration has been built with high-frequency and high-capacity needs in mind, making it ideal for moving many people between cities in the Southeast Asia region. Premium passengers have access to 36 business class seats in a 1-2-1 configuration, while the rest of the aircraft is taken up by a massive 301-seat economy cabin. Singapore Airlines has no premium economy class available on this jet. Just one row in this economy cabin is marked as extra legroom.

The table below shows the dimensions of the seats in each of the two cabins, making use of Seat Maps data:

20 inches (51 cm) - expands when armrests are lowered

36 seats is admittedly a substantial business-class cabin, which seems to go against the high-capacity, short-haul philosophy this article has focused on. However, Singapore Airlines uses a staggered layout that squeezes in these seats in a relatively small area. Furthermore, although these seats are lie-flat, they have a shorter pitch and narrower width than what business-class passengers would usually expect.

Despite the prevalence of routes under 2,000 miles (3,200 km), there are still some routes that get closer to the typical way in which carriers use widebody aircraft. In November, Singapore Airlines will fly its 787-10s over 3,000 miles (4,830 km) on four routes. These will be Adelaide Airport (ADL), Tokyo Narita International Airport (NRT), Chubu Centrair International Airport (NGO) in Nagoya, and Osaka's Kansai International Airport (KIX).

Notably, only one of these routes takes the 787-10 outside Asia, and the longer routes within Asia largely reflect Japan's relative isolation from the rest of the continent. The longest of these four routes is to ADL, covering 3,351 miles (5,400 km). Singapore Airlines will complete this route 52 times in November, for a total of 17,524 seats and 58.7 million available seat miles. In 2024, Singapore Airlines marked 40 years of its presence in the city. The airline first flew there in 1984, 18 months after ADL's international terminal opened, using a 747. Along with the 787, Singapore Airlines also uses the A350 for this route, which began in October 2023.

The highest-frequency route, over 3,000 miles, is to KIX, with 90 frequencies across November. This produces 30,330 seats at an average distance of 3,040 miles (4,900 km), producing over 92 million available seat miles. That total is the highest for any Singapore Airlines Dreamliner route.

But it is not the large variant's longest passenger-carrying route of all time.

Relying on a single aircraft type to operate a specific route will always leave an airline vulnerable. This could become a problem for Singapore Airlines with a Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) airworthiness directive coming into effect next month. It covers all 787 variants and was instituted because sealant defects could create a spark inside the jet's fuel tanks during a lightning strike. "The unsafe condition, if not addressed, could result in development of an ignition source within the fuel tank volume," states the FAA order.

The prevalence of this issue and the extent of the problem it will cause remain unclear. The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) says that 60 of the 170 US-registered 787s fall under the directive, requiring safety inspections. Singapore Airlines' Dreamliners do not fall under this directive, but could be affected if a problem exists for Dreamliners worldwide. The FAA says that impacted jets need sealant inspections where the wings meet the fuselage, along with adhesion checks. FAA predictions put the work for each jet at 34 hours, costing $2,890 per jet. Further costs will be incurred where repairs are required. With over 1,100 Dreamliners worldwide, the problem could cause substantial disruption across global aviation.

Elsewhere in Singapore Airlines' widebody fleet, Boeing problems are causing other issues. Most notably, the carrier has an outstanding order for 31 Boeing 777X jets, placed in June 2017 and February 2021. It has no options for additional purchases. This aircraft family has been long-delayed by certification issues, although Boeing insists that certification and deliveries will finally come in 2027. Given the aircraft's huge size, its arrival should further cement the need for Singapore Airlines to use its Dreamliners on short, high-capacity routes.

Here's What the Average Man's Social Security Benefit Looks Like by Age

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Men's average retirement benefit runs $409.85 higher monthly than women's, a gap rooted in lifetime earnings with survivor benefit implications.

Men's average Social Security benefits run higher than women's at every age. This breakdown lays out the specific figures across the early retirement years based on the most recent data snapshot.

As of December 2025, the average retirement benefit among men is $2,282.28 per month, according to the Social Security Administration (SSA). The average for women is $1,872.43, which leaves a monthly gap of $409.85.

The SSA bases your benefit on an average of your wages across your 35 highest-earning years. These wages are adjusted for inflation and run through a formula designed to replace about 40% of your pre-retirement income. Most retirees need a much higher share of their prior pay to cover their costs.

These averages represent a snapshot of current beneficiaries, including those who claimed early and those who waited for higher payments. As of December 2025, the monthly averages show consistent differences between genders across the board.

The higher average for men reflects historical differences in lifetime earnings and years spent in the paid workforce. While individual work histories vary, these broader patterns shape the current benefit landscape across every age group.

A man who is 65 today is expected to live about 18 more years, while a woman is expected to live about 21 more years, according to the SSA. This means that men's Social Security benefits typically cover a larger proportion of their retirement costs than women's, who have to pay for more years of living expenses with smaller checks.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027. The above figures don't yet reflect that increase, which will add about $91 a month to the $2,529.62 average for 70-year-old men. Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later.

Married people have the option to claim a spousal benefit on their partner's work record while their partner is still alive and a survivor benefit after their partner's death. You only get these checks if they're worth more than your own retirement benefit.

Since women have historically had fewer years in the workforce and lower average earnings, they're more likely to claim spousal and survivor benefits, which means married men also need to think about how their claiming decision could affect their spouse.

Your partner cannot claim a spousal benefit until you are receiving your retirement checks, so your claiming age may affect theirs. Talk your options over with your spouse and make sure you're both on the same page about when each of you plans to sign up. If one of you wants to change things up down the road, keep the other in the loop and adjust your retirement plan accordingly.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

Air Algerie Business Class Airbus A330neo Review: Utterly Awful

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For the first long haul segment of my Orient Express adventure via Africa trip, my first class quest to Japan, I flew Air Algerie’s Airbus A330-900neo business class on the 6hr50min flight from Montreal (YUL) to Algiers (ALG). I had heard almost entirely negative things about Air Algerie, which intrigued me, naturally! Well, now that I’ve flown Air Algerie I can confirm that everything I heard was true, and then some.

I don’t want to be dramatic — obviously flying business class is better than economy — but honestly, just about everything sucked. On the plus side, the seats as such were fine, but that’s where the good news ends. The food was not good, and took an insanely long amount of time to be served. There were no amenity kits… or menus… or meal choice… or alcohol… or diet soda. If the crew were any more indifferent, they would’ve been invisible.

I wouldn’t fly Air Algerie again unless I absolutely had to, which… I can’t imagine that’ll ever be the case. Honestly, Air Algerie made me yearn for my recent flight on Turkmenistan Airlines, which was at least fun.

Air Algerie is a pretty isolated airline, which perhaps reflects Algeria overall — the country just isn’t interested in visitors, and getting a visa requires jumping through hoops.

So Air Algerie doesn’t have any useful partnerships when it comes to earning or redeeming miles. As a result, booking a cash ticket is just about the only option, unless you’re a frequent flyer with the airline. Air Algerie tends to have rather steep business class fares, despite its lackluster product.

However, I did manage to find a rather attractive one-way business class fare. I booked the following in business class for $1,171 in cash:

10/03 AH2701 Montreal to Algiers departing 4:10PM arriving 5:00AM (+1 day)10/04 AH4000 Algiers to Tunis departing 7:10AM arriving 8:30AM

The first segment was operated by an Airbus A330-900neo, while the second segment was operated by an Airbus A320 that was wet leased from Avion Express.

I had flown into Montreal the same day from Miami (MIA) on Air Canada Rouge, so I had an extended layover. I was at the Air Algerie check-in counter before it opened at 12:30PM, and there was already quite the queue of passengers waiting. Unsurprisingly, and as is very common for North Africa flights, people had a ton of checked bags.

It took me around 30 minutes to get checked in, since there was one counter for business class, and there were half a dozen people in front of me. At first I was asked if I had a visa for Algeria, though I explained I was in transit, which seemed to catch the check-in agent off guard. Air Algerie is clearly targeted at the local community, and doesn’t see much international connecting traffic.

Security was a breeze, and Air Algerie sends its business class passengers to the National Bank Lounge, which is the contract lounge in the international portion of the terminal. I won’t do a full review, but it was actually a pretty pleasant facility, with plenty of seating, lots of natural light, and it wasn’t very crowded.

The food selection was pretty average, with a handful of hot options, plus cheese, cold cuts, etc. There was coffee, tea, wine, beer, and soft drinks. I made the mistake of saving my hunger and thirst for onboard the Air Algerie flight!

My Air Algerie flight was departing from gate A58, with boarding scheduled for 3:10PM, a full hour before departure. I was at the gate early enough to see the inbound aircraft arrive, and goodness, what a stunner it was!

The Montreal route is primarily served by an A330neo with the registration code 7T-VLB, which is in a special livery celebrating the country’s football team. Looking at the plane was honestly the best part of the flight experience, unfortunately.

The boarding area got very full before departure, with lots of passengers in wheelchairs, and lots of crowding around the gate. Boarding ended up starting at 3:20PM, with those needing extra time, as well as business class passengers, being invited to board first.

I can’t describe how excited I was to step onboard, as I always love trying new airlines! I wasn’t fully prepared for the level of indifference shown by every part of the experience, though.

Before I get into this review, I should mention that Air Algerie has recently started taking delivery of Airbus A330-900neos as part of its fleet modernization initiative. However, the airline isn’t just taking delivery of these planes factory fresh, but has also acquired a couple secondhand from Air Belgium, which went out of business in 2024.

Air Algerie maintained the Air Belgium interiors — they also have staggered seats, but it’s a slightly different configuration. I boarded through the second door on the left, and then turned left into the business class cabin.

Business class takes up the entire space between the first and second set of doors, and consists of 30 seats, spread across eight rows, in a 1-2-1 configuration. These seats are from the Stelia Aerospace Solstys line, so they’re typical last generation staggered business class seats. When it comes to the seat details, they have 44″ of pitch, 21″ of width, and convert into 77″ flat beds.

On the plus side, it’s really easy to photograph Air Algerie cabins, since crews are nowhere to be seen. 😉

If you’re traveling with someone, you may appreciate the sets of center seats in odd numbered rows, as these are “honeymoon” seats, which allow you to sit really close to your companion. Meanwhile in even numbered rows, the seats in the center section are further from one another and really close to the aisles, so those would be my least preferred seats in this configuration.

Along the windows, seats alternate between being closer to the aisle and closer to the windows. In odd numbered rows, seats are closer to the aisle. What I particularly don’t like about these staggered seats is just how exposed some of them are to the aisle, as you really have no separation.

In even numbered rows, seats are closer to the windows, and these are the best seats in the house, in terms of offering some separation from the aisle, and an easy view out the window.

I assigned myself seat 6A, the window seat on the left side in the third to last row.

These staggered seats have a console to the side of the seat, and on the side of that is a panel with seat controls, an entertainment controller, and a small armrest.

There’s an exposed storage area above the panel, where you can store things, or even hang the headphones. As you can see, the seat just wasn’t well maintained, and also wasn’t clean, as it also needed a good wiping down.

In terms of charging, the AC power outlet and USB-A outlet were located underneath the seat. There was no USB-C or wireless charging, unsurprisingly.

Looking at the front of the seat, the tray table is stored behind the seat in front, and can be extended by being lowered and then moved over. One of the nice things about these tray tables is that you can angle them away from you, so it’s possible to get up during the meal service.

The entertainment system is in a fixed position and is located above the ottoman. The monitor here was 16″ and was touchscreen, but wasn’t terribly high definition.

As much as there are many aspects of this seat I don’t love, I do like how big the footwell is, which is great for those of us with big feet. It’s not necessarily that comfortable when you’re laying sideways in terms of space for knees, but still, there was a great amount of space here.

One other interesting note is that there were no overhead bins in the center of the cabin, so the overhead bins along the windows got pretty full, since the A330neo doesn’t have huge bins. On top of that, there were no individual air nozzles above seats, sadly. That was a problem on this flight, given how warm the cabin was kept.

All-in-all, Air Algerie’s business class hard product is okay. The cabin maintenance could definitely use some help, but honestly, this is the least of Air Algerie’s problems.

The Air Algerie business class entertainment system on the A330neo is pretty awful. For one, there’s not even a map feature. I’m sorry, I’m not sure the last time I flew an airline with seat back entertainment that didn’t have a map feature. What’s going on there?

The system did have some movies, TV shows, and music, but it was a really lackluster selection.

In total, there were 100 movies and 36 TV shows, but it definitely felt like the number of “popular” options was limited.

The airline presumably went with the cheapest entertainment package available. For example, when it comes to sitcoms, there was one episode of “Fresh Off The Boat,” and it was the pilot episode from 2015. Yikes.

When it comes to Wi-Fi, that’s something that just isn’t offered by Air Algerie. Funny enough, these ex-Air Belgium A330neos have Wi-Fi installed, but the airline just chooses not to use the system. So expect that you’re going to disconnect when flying with Air Algerie.

When it comes to amenities in Air Algerie business class, they were also very limited. The highlight was the pillow and blanket, which were at the seat upon boarding, but not exactly very nicely presented.

Anyway, this was the highlight of the amenities, and while they were nothing amazing, they were at least decent.

Headphones were also passed out well after takeoff, though the quality of them was very poor. This was closer to what you’d expect in economy than business class.

There were no amenity kits, or anything else to speak of. I don’t remember the last overnight business class flight I’ve taken where I wasn’t offered an amenity kit?

Despite the very drawn out boarding process, the crew was nowhere to be seen for most of boarding. Finally at 4:10PM, right at departure time, they came around with pre-departure drinks. There was no “hello” or “welcome onboard,” but instead, just the tray being held out with orange juice or water. At least it was served in glassware… yay?

Boarding this Air Algerie A330neo took an incredibly long time. Every seat was taken (business class seemed to be occupied mostly by operational upgrades plus friends and family). There was just endless confusion, and the crew’s only involvement with things was to occasionally check on people they knew and have chats with them, while everyone else was ignored.

Boarding was also rough because it seemed like the APU was broken, so the cabin was incredibly hot. During boarding, I had asked the flight attendant if I could use the lavatory (I try to do that as a courtesy, even though it showed as unoccupied), and she responded “wait.” I checked with her again some time later, and she again said “wait.” I didn’t end up getting to go before departure… oh well!

Our departure time came and went, and about 20 minutes later I asked a flight attendant if we were delayed. She didn’t understand my question, but after rephrasing, her answer was “no.” The door ended up closing at 4:35PM, 25 minutes behind the scheduled departure time.

At that point the manual safety demonstration began, which was exclusively performed in French and Arabic, and not in English. In fairness, I was probably the only person onboard who didn’t have French or Arabic as my first language. I should also mention there were no safety checks during taxi — some people had their seats reclined, plenty of people still had their (glass) pre-departure beverages, and the guy across from me didn’t even have his seatbelt on.

We began our pushback at 4:40PM, and then at 4:50PM we began our taxi.

While taxiing (and turning a corner), the captain made his welcome onboard announcement at 4:55PM, telling us “this is captain, good morning, taking off shortly.” Okay, we’ll ignore the “morning” part (I’ll assume it just means he’s well rested!), but I assume at Air Algerie the captain is also the one taxiing, right? If so, that’s a lot of multitasking, especially when you’re actively turning.

But hey, credit where credit’s due, this guy was good at turning corners. When we got to runway 24R at 5PM and were cleared for takeoff, we didn’t slowly make the turn and then accelerate. Instead, we started accelerating pretty aggressively in our turn, and were going over 15 knots, while turning at a 90 degree angle. Seriously, check the Flightradar24 data.

We had a very powerful takeoff roll and the captain pulled up on the sidestick as if we were in a fighter jet. The views on the climb out were gorgeous — what a lovely afternoon!

We made a sharp turn after takeoff, and had some nice views of the airport thanks to that.

The seatbelt sign was turned off around five minutes after takeoff. A little while later, we started to get a beautiful sunset view as well.

Dinner was served after takeoff in Air Algerie business class, but my gosh, what a bad and drawn out meal it was. I can deal with bad food or slow service, but bad food, bad drinks, and slow service? BLEH. The first thing to understand is that there were no menus or choices. You’re just served stuff, and it’s a “take it or leave it” situation.

Service began 50 minutes after takeoff, when warm towels were distributed.

Then around 15 minutes later, tablecloths were handed out. These felt more like large, flimsy napkins, than actual tablecloths, but whatever, no biggie.

Another 10 minutes later, the first course was served. The actual food was a little smoked salmon, some egg, some olives, etc. It didn’t taste good at all, but what was worse was the presentation. It’s as if they just threw a bunch of packaging on the tray, and put zero effort into how it’s presented.

I couldn’t help but do their job for them, and make it look a little nicer, because it’s just embarrassing. Oh, and bread is just served by hand out of the basket. Nice.

I kind of loved the Air Algerie branding on the cutlery, but that’s the same cutlery you have to use for the entire meal. For that matter, the cutlery was downright sticky when I removed it from the packaging, so clearly it just hadn’t been cleaned properly.

Drinks were brought out separately from the meal, but they might as well not have bothered. I didn’t realize that Air Algerie is a “dry” airline — it hadn’t even made my list of airlines that are dry. As it turns out, this is only a fairly recent development, as the airline switched to being dry a few years ago.

It’s not only that they don’t have alcohol, but they don’t even have any diet sodas. They have juice or full calorie soda. There was also a huge language barrier here:

“Drink?”“Do you have wine?”“Soda, juice.”“Do you have Diet Coke?”“Coke?”“No, Diet Coke.”“What?”“Coke Zero? Diet Pepsi? Pepsi Max?”*stares blankly*“I’ll have a water, please.”*serves me Sprite*

Another 15 minutes later the main course was served. Again, there was no option, and it seemed to be some sort of a swordfish, or something. As someone who typically loves fish (and orders it way too often on planes), I found this to be way overcooked, dry, and completely tasteless. If you’re not going to offer people a choice of meals, what a random thing to serve!

After an additional pause, the crew came around with cheese plates. I mean, they certainly serve quite a quantity of food, the issue is just that there are no choices, and none of it is actually good. They also don’t actually clear any plates. They just keep bringing more and more food, and if your tray is overflowing, so be it (for the below picture, I had placed the other stuff from the tray over on the side console).

Next up, two whole apples were served, which… I mean, I can’t blame them for the excessive amount of food, serving cheese, fruit, and dessert. But still, this very much feels like an afterthought. I also couldn’t help but wonder if those apples had been washed, given that we’re talking about an airline that serves bread by hand.

Lastly there was a little dessert tart trio. I tried to order a tea without caffeine, but that was a challenge:

“Coffee or tea?”“Do you have any tea without caffeine?”“Coffee or tea?”“Tea please, but no caffeine.”“Coffee?”“Tea, please.”

The entire meal service took close to three hours. Nothing about it was good. The food wasn’t good. The service was terrible. I mean, everything was just so poorly timed. There was never even the offer for more drinks (even just bottled water), and when I did ask, it took them 15 minutes and multiple reminders to actually get some more water.

I just don’t get it. As another example, they served the cheese plates, and then 10 minutes later they came around with more bread, after just about everyone had finished their cheese course. I’m exhausted writing about this. I need a drink.

The Air Algerie A330neo business class lavatory is located at the very front of the cabin. One lavatory really isn’t enough for a full business class cabin plus the crew, so there was often a wait. While there are two lavatories behind the cabin, they’re shared with economy passengers, so there’s usually a line.

The lavatories were fine. Toward the end of the flight they definitely weren’t clean, but to be honest, I’ve seen much worse lavatories on airlines in the region, so I’m hardly going to complain.

By the time the meal service was complete, we had well under four hours remaining to Algiers. So I reclined my seat into bed mode, and tried to get a bit of rest. The bed was okay, and again, compared to the rest of the experience, it was one of the better things.

The issue is how hard it was to actually get rest on this flight. The lights were fully on until after the meal service was complete, so at that point it was under four hours until our arrival in Algiers.

Then the cabin lights were once again fully turned on exactly two hours before arrival. So for those keeping track at home, on a 6hr50min flight, the lights were dimmed for around 1hr50min. I’m not sure I actually slept for a minute. The cabin was really warm, and with the lack of individual air nozzles, I just couldn’t get comfortable.

It absolutely boggles my mind why the crew has to start the pre-landing service a full two hours before landing, when the extent of the breakfast is a single tray with a small side of fruit, a small piece of cake, and a croissant. Seriously, this is what everyone is being woken up two hours before landing for?!

Credit where credit’s due, the drip coffee with milk was drinkable, and perhaps the culinary highlight of the flight. Getting a refill was beyond my abilities, but I sure did savor that one cup!

I understand that North African countries are often known for warm hospitality in a non-transactional setting, where there’s a genuine warmth and generosity to hospitality. Clearly that doesn’t apply in a transactional setting, though, and certainly not on Air Algerie.

I don’t want to say the crew was rude — that wouldn’t be fair, and I didn’t even communicate with them enough to draw that conclusion. They actually seemed to be very nice in how they chatted with their friends who were in the cabin.

As a customer, though, everything about the service was terrible. The crew had no warmth, no attentiveness, they were incredibly disorganized and slow, and the service flow just made no sense. If I were to train a crew on how to provide good service in business class, I’d have them experience this flight, and then have them do exactly the opposite. I think I’ve ragged on them enough.

You’ve gotta love how the refreshing towel has Air Algerie’s logo, and then says “Always caring for you.” If this is what care looks like, I’d love to see what not being cared for looks like!

I was excited when my Air Algerie flight was slowly coming to an end. At around 4:20AM Algiers time the seatbelt sign was turned on, and the crew prepared the cabin for landing. Now, that didn’t actually involve doing any security checks, but they made sure passengers were aware there would be no more service.

We ended up touching down at 4:50AM, and it must’ve been one of the smoothest touchdowns of my life, as you could barely feel it. From there, we had a short five minute taxi to a remote stand, where we pulled in at 4:55AM.

It’s very odd how an airport that has so many empty gates and that’s rather underutilized sends its “flagship” aircraft to remote stands, but whatever.

Upon deplaning, there wasn’t so much as a “goodbye” from the crew. There also wasn’t any priority deplaning for business class, or a separate business class bus. It was quite a long drive to the terminal, and we got there at 5:15AM, where my interesting transit experience started…

I highly recommend flying Air Algerie business class… if you want to be reminded how every other airline doesn’t actually suck that much, by comparison. Air Algerie’s A330neo business class hard product is okay, and is the least of the issues.

The problem is everything else, from the completely indifferent crew, to the unnecessarily drawn out service, to the lack of meal options, amenity kits, menus, diet soda, alcohol, etc., to the subpar entertainment and lack of Wi-Fi… the list goes on and on. And that says nothing of the bizarre elements of the flight, like no English safety demos, no safety checks before takeoff or landing, etc.

What’s sad is that I would’ve actually had a more favorable impression of the flight if they just said “this is a sleeper flight, and we have no inflight service.” Truly, no service would’ve been better than this. I’m happy I had the chance to fly Air Algerie, but you know, I think I’m good for the time being!

What do you make of Air Algerie’s business class?

‘Passing Strange’ Comes to Pasadena Playhouse: Creators Stew and Heidi Rodewald on the Rock Musical Reaching L.A. Almost Two Decades After Tony-Winning Broadway Run

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Senior Music Writer and Chief Music Critic

“Passing Strange” will seem just a little less strange to theatergoers now than it might have when it opened on Broadway in 2007 and scored big wins at the Tonys, the Drama Desk Awards and the New York Drama Critics’ Circle Awards. Without naming any names, there are plenty of successful shows since then that have taken cues from the rock musical, from how it put social and racial issues up front for its fouth-wall-breaking protagonist to having a live band on stage. Related Stories Paramount Network Orders 13 Original Episodes of Larry the Cable Guy-Hosted Clip Show 'American Mayhem' (EXCLUSIVE)

The only truly strange thing, maybe, at this late date? Why it took so long for the show to get an L.A. production. After all, Stew and his creative partner Heidi Rodewald used to be prominent figures on the Los Angeles music scene, as members of the critically hailed band the Negro Problem, before the development of this show took them to New York in the early 2000s. So they’ve always a loyal audience of rock fans in SoCal eagerly waiting for the show to take root here, too, on top of Angeleno theater buffs who never got to see the show back east. Now it’s finally happening, thanks to director Zhailon Levingston, who won the Tony this year for best direction of a musical for “Cats: The Jellicle Ball.” When he sat down with the top folks at Pasadena Playhouse to discuss what he might like to do here, giving “Passing Strange” its proper SoCal premiere was at the top of his list.

Stew and Rodewald will not be performing in the show in Pasadena, as they did throughout its entire Broadway and pre-Broadway runs. (Neither will Colman Domingo, who had a supporting role, pre-fame, on Broadway. You can see all of those performances in the film version Spike Lee directed.) Vintage Trouble singer Ty Taylor stars in this production as the Stew stand-in known only as Narrator, along with Brittany Campbell (pictured above), Britton Smith and Saycon Sengbloh. Stew and Rodewald are as eager as anyone to see what Levingston has done with a project that takes elements of Stew’s real coming-of-age and sets them to rock ‘n’ roll. The pair Zoomed in with Variety to discuss the history and impact of the show, as well as reveal some projects now in the works.

The two of you aren’t directly working on this Pasadena Playhouse direction, but more like watching appreciatively from afar how it’s going, right?

Stew: Oh, yeah. No, we wouldn’t dare. Zhailon Levingston was sweet enough at one point to invite us to peek in, but he’s a total visionary, so all we could do at this point would be drag it down. First he knows the play better than we do. Secondly, if you’ve ever seen his work, like the work he did in “Cats: The Jellicle Ball,” he’s honestly the last person I would give any advice to.

Rodewald: The way he talks about “Passing Strange” just makes us think: Whatever you’re going to do, you get it.

Stew: As I was just telling him just a few minutes ago, it was made for him. We don’t make art for the contemporary world. The stuff that’s moved me and Heidi for the most part has always been those early morning moments when you go home with somebody and they wanna show you that they’re cool, so they put on a record that you’ve never heard before. I call that 3 a.m. music, and I think that’s what we make. I think the Negro Problem are a 3 a.m. band. “Passing Strange” is a 3 a.m. musical. It’s not something you’re gonna get fed by the mainstream, but somebody very cool is gonna turn you onto it. You don’t necessarily make a lot of money that way, but that’s not why we’re in this, obviously.

Here in L.A., remembering your band as an L.A. band originally, I remember hearing of the show when it was first playing at the Public and then coming to Broadway, thinking that it’d be great to see when you brought it to L.A. I couldn’t have realized that would be a 22-year journey.

Stew: I know. We didn’t either. “Passing Strange” has been done in almost every major city except L.A. It’s been done in South America; it’s been done in Europe — so, yeah, it was starting to stick out in a kind of a weird way. But, see, we’ve always had a adversarial relationship with both Los Angeles and to the L.A. press, particularly the L.A. Times. So we were sort of not surprised at all, but other people would always come to us and go, “What’s up? The play won a Tony! Why aren’t you guys in your hometown?” And we’re like, “Uh, I don’t know.” … It’s certainly nothing we’ve lost sleep over. Having a London production to me was a dream come true, so it’s all good. But the good news is that this amazing director is doing it in L.A., so who cares about time? It’s gonna be worth the wait.

Going back to the very beginnings of the show, weren’t you kind of developing some kind of preliminary version of it in clubs?

Stew: Yeah, we had this thing called “Travelogue” that we did at some places in New York, which was sort of a cabaret version of us talking about different places that we had lived and different experiences — a very primitive version in Joe’s Pub and a couple of other clubs where we did stuff in New York, and that’s when the word started to get around. We were approached by the Public Theater. We weren’t petitioning to make a play. They approached us and said, “Would you like to make it a theater (piece)?” We said no three times, and then finally said yes.

You’ve said that Broadway was not an aspiration of yours, ever. It was not your dream come true.

Rodewald: No, and that’s why it ended up being what it is, because we weren’t thinking that way at all. We weren’t trying to please people. We were just pleasing ourselves. I think we really held onto that. … It was this crazy surreal thing. And then we did have choices of doing it a different way or being on Broadway, and we went with that. We didn’t have to go to Broadway; we could have been one of those cool shows that goes on and on forever, off-Broadway. But it wasn’t like “Oh my God, this is all we’ve ever wanted.”

Stew: It really helped being a rock band, because I guess there must be some rock bands that get really overly excited about things, but I think a lot of rock bands are like us: we’re very jaded. Don’t forget, we’re old enough to have experienced the implosion of the music business itself. So we remember the point where people were trying to get record deals, and then we also were there when people are like, “Fuck a record deal. Let’s make the record ourselves.” Which is what we did.

We were in the office with Lenny Waronker, talking about a record deal, and they did give us $10,000. Woo, right? And we took that $10,000 and instead of making their demos, we took that $10,000 and made our record with it. So technically our second record is a DreamWorks project, but they don’t know it. Their accountant probably knows it, but they don’t.

At what point did you go east from L.A. to New York?

Stew: Yeah, we were playing in New York a lot when we were touring a lot, but I guess 2000 was probably where we really started spending more time. The best thing you can do in New York is not tell people you moved there. When you’ve moved there, you tell people you’re just visiting so they treat you better.

Rodewald: We haven’t talked about this in a long time. With “Passing Strange,” we get this commission, and then we’re working at the Public Theater, and they’re sending us away to do workshops and it’s like, “Wow, they’re giving us money, so we can just keep going.” In the middle of this, Stew and I are still making albums and touring, so we’re not banking everything on it; we’re just doing this other thing. And one of the great things that happened was, by the time Broadway happened, we still didn’t officially live in New York, so they had to put us up. Otherwise, how would we have done that?

Stew: Sort of like all the luxuries we never got with a big record deal, suddenly theater people were giving it to us, and they were much nicer.

Rodewald: At one point, we were at Sundance, and I remember looking at you and going, “They actually care! Like, no, they actually do.”

And that was not your experience with the record business.

Stew: I feel like record business was just a lot of suits pretending that they get you, but not really. Theater people are really into lyrics, for instance. So that for us was something very astonishing. People liked our stage show and our outlandish name and our ambitious music or whatever, but people in L.A. didn’t really say much about lyrics. Some people did, but not like press and stuff. Then suddenly we go to New York, and the theater people are like, “Whoa, we love these lyrics.”

Also, it’s a touchy subject because I know a lot of our L.A. people feel funny about the fact that we really did have to go to New York in order to make a living from this. Because we were selling out Spaceland on Saturday night, but there was a glass ceiling, and we couldn’t get that big record deal that was no longer around, or no longer around for a band like us. That wasn’t a possibility anymore. Suddenly in New York we had a career and could make a living. It’s always weird when hometown people leave and go elsewhere, and things happen.

I have a lot of friends, and Heidi has a lot of friends, who, back in the day, would get a big advance from a record company, and they’d be crawling the carpets with all the cocaine and Gil Turner (high-end liquor) deliveries. And the thing is, theater doesn’t give you Gil Turner money. It doesn’t give you cocaine money up front. But it does provide you with everything else you need to create. “We’re gonna send you away on a nice retreat in the cool countryside or the hills or Berlin.” They give you everything you need in assistance, but you’re not gonna become a coke addict on a theater budget. And that’s good!

Rodewald: It was amazing, but kind of embarrassing, to tell peole, “Well, we have a week, and we’re working on this, and we’re getting paid fairly.”

Let’s talk about the impact “Passing Strange” had on Broadway. Did you arrive at a point where you were able to recognize the influence it had?

Stew: For sure. After us, there were two things that were very clear. One is that every show wanted to have a band on stage. That was almost immediate. And the micro thing that only theater people would know is our lighting design, which was considered so weird at the time. Our lighting designer was like, “I want to use these lights that’ve been rejected from, like, seven shows.” And we saw it, and it looked like “Liza With a Z,” and we were like, “Hell yeah, we want this.” And then suddenly everybody started copying that look once they saw it. But that stuff isn’t really that big a deal. When I say “of course we would be influential,” I don’t mean that as from an egotistical point. Obviously, when you introduce something new, artists wanna check it out and play with it.

But I don’t think our influence was deep enough, if anything, to be honest. I still think musicals are pretty un-rocking and kind of shallow. And when they say “rock musical,” that just means there’s gonna be a scene where you hear a fuzzy guitar turned up.

Do you know the Iggy Pop thing where he’s [at a show in Cincinnati], he’s covering the peanut butter [all over his body] and stage diving into the audience? I show that to all my theater students, and I say, “For me, this is the epitome of rock ‘n’ roll. Why? Because we don’t know what’s going to happen next, and he is in completely in that moment. He’s high as a motherfucker, but he is completely in that moment.” And that’s what we want to achieve in rock ‘n’ roll. We want to be so present and so there that you want to come there with us. And then I ask them, “Can you show me moments in musical theater that are like this?” And when they do, they are usually scenes that are not even approaching that level of nakedness and frankness and vulnerability.

So what I try to do in my class is get people to write musicals that are more rock ‘n’ roll. And I don’t mean rock ‘n’ roll music necessarily; I mean the spirit. So when you talk about “Passing Strange’s” influence, I mean, superficially, sure. But I don’t know if it’s been deeper than that. Maybe with some shows it has. I don’t know.

Rodewald: The thing that really hit us at one point… We were touring doing some shows after that, onto the next thing. We’re playing in Detroit and most of the people there were like, “Oh, I saw that show.” When you think about how many shows we’ve played live as a band across all these years, [that didn’t compare with] we then do this show where it’s 1,200 people, eight shows a week. We didn’t go on forever, but when you think of that many people, everywhere we go, they’re like, “Oh my God, I saw you on Broadway.” And yeah, a lot of people in theater will be like, “That’s from ‘Passing Strange.'” It’s really amazing to be part of that world where people have been really influenced — a lot of people.

Stew: Most rock people who are involved in musicals are already really famous. Like Green Day. Or it’s like, “Okay, we’re big. We got nothing else to do. Let’s make a musical,” that kind of vibe. But I feel like what was really cool for us is we weren’t anywhere near that. We were indie famous, if even that. We were able to be ourselves in theater and individual in theater because of that. We weren’t trying to please any fans; we were just trying to please ourselves.

Do you think it broke conventions as well as helped invent some new ones?

Rodewald: I’m thinking back on different workshops over the years, these amazing workshops like Sundance. And as we’re doing the rehearsals, we’re laughing, going, “And if we were on Broadway, we would do this.” At the end of the song, everybody claps, and then the music comes back as everybody marches off the stage, just all those kinds of things. We would just laugh about it the whole time, right? We were well aware of what those things were that we did not want to do. And we had a lot of of conversations about wanting to give people a chance to clap, where we were like, “No, no buttons” —that kind of stuff.

In a video interview you did with Spike Lee right he filmed the last show, you indicated you were glad you were through with starring in it in certain ways, and you didn’t really imagine other people doing it. You didn’t see it being the kind of show where somebody just stepped into your role.

I totally didn’t, yeah. I remember the very first one we saw in D.C. And I thought, “Wow, this is awesome,” because that’s the cool thing about theater — it’s just like doing a cover. You know, the best covers don’t steal completely? The best covers do their own thing. So that was what was really fun. The narrator in the first (Washington) show was completely different than me, and I thought it was fantastic. So, yeah, the different people that have done it… We got a call from Argentina, where they were doing it, and they were like, “We’re thinking about not doing ‘The Black One’ because we just feel like that’d be kind of weird with a bunch of white Argentinian actors. Are you OK with that?” And I’m like, “Yeaaaaah, I’m okay with that.” Same when they did it in Belgium. So yeah, it’s just been fun for us. Heidi said it best — it’s bitter and… what did you say?

Rodewald: Oh, well, I feel like I say that a lot: “Old enough and bitter enough.”

Stew: It’s “old and bitter,” but in a very fun, devil-may-care kind of way… I think we enjoy what has happened, but we never sat around and were like, “Oh my God, this is it.” Because to us, it isn’t; it’s just we just do this. This is our life.

You don’t have a huge, active social media presence where you’re promoting yourselves, so it would be good to catch up on what you have coming up. For one thing, does the band still do concerts?

Stew: Oh, absolutely. Negro Problem will come out with a record in ’27. And we’re constantly writing and doing things. Heidi’s writing this amazing show about Aileen Wuornos. What is it? Is it like a song cycle, or is it gonna be a actual musical?

Rodewald: Well, the beautiful thing about it in theater is: I don’t know yet. We’re writing the songs, and then we’re gonna figure it out. But it’s not gonna be straightforward.

Stew: And she and I have this thing called Stewdio Zero. That’s this thing where, every four months, we make a show, and we collaborate with former students of mine from The New School, from Harvard, from LaGuardia. So it’s us being the age we are, but there’s also six people that are like a third of our age on stage collaborating to make sure things stay fresh. We perform at a wonderful space in New York called Jack, which is like our home. And, yeah, we’re crazy busy. We’re making a film of this show we did on Harlem Stage called “High Substitute for the Head Lecturer.” It’s about Amiri Baraka and Allen Ginsberg. I’m working on a Broadway-bound thing on both Muhammad Ali and Thelonious Monk.

Rodewald: “Broadway-bound.” That’s amazing. I love that you said that.

Stew: Yeah, bound in the sense of like prison. And Heidi, you’re doing something else too, right? What are you doing?

Rodewald: Well, I’m gonna be doing the Grace and Jimmy Boggs project with you.

Stew: Oh, yeah — we just got the money for this project about these legendary activists, Grace Lee Boggs and Jimmy Boggs. We’re gonna do this really, I hope, epic live song cycle about these two truly revolutionary activists out of Detroit. They’re already well known to political people, but I hope this musical we’re gonna make about them puts them on the map. They were unapologetically radical and wonderfully charismatic people. We’re busy as fuck. And I teach at Harvard, which is insanely busy, you know?

Are you teaching theater or music at Harvard, or both?

Stew: Both. My class should be titled “How to Make Musical Theater That Doesn’t Suck,” but they wouldn’t let me title it that. So it’s called “Singer Plus Song Equals Story,” and I just try to get them to make musicals that are more informed by what I would consider a rock ‘n’ roll ethos, more than “let’s put on a show” kind of stuff. The students are incredible, and this is what the Stewdio Zero group does, too, that Heidi and I have with them. They kind of teach us what’s going on now, and then we get to tell them what happened. We get to tell them about this little thing we like to call the 20th century, beause they know nothing about that, if you could believe.

Rodewald: The thing that’s changed for me is… Stew insists… I used to have to say “Tony Award-winning Stew,” like, throw that in. [Stew won the Tony for best book for a musical.] And now sometimes he makes me call him “the Professor.”

Stew: I admitted in front of a class that there was a point in my life where I didn’t know the difference between Miley Cyrus, Katy Perry, and… what’s her name, the other one? Taylor Swift. The level of eye roll that happened among my students, it was like teaching zombies. Their eyes turned white because their eyes went so far up into their head, and they groaned this zombie-like groan. I think they all had to go to the eye doctor after I said this. They just could not believe that I couldn’t tell the difference between those three. I never tell them that I still kinda can’t if I’m just listening. This is so embarrassing, because I realize that they’re all fine. They’re great. They even have songs that I like. But when someone comes up to me and goes, “Yeah, I’m really into songwriting, like John Lennon and Taylor Swift,” you know, I just… I’m not criticizing that. I know it’s okay. I’m just not there yet, you know what I mean? Maybe by the time I’m 80. I’m 65 now. When I’m 80, I think it’ll be a Taylor Swift light bulb, you know what I mean? I’m working towards it. I’m working.
