12 HBO Max Shows That Are Perfect From Start To Finish

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

TV fans don't have to look any further than HBO Max to find series that are constantly raising the bar for what we see on television. The best TV shows hold the audience's attention from start to finish, never disappointing viewers or dropping the ball by failing to follow through on plotlines and character arcs. When a TV series is so good that it's considered perfect, it's clear that viewers should take notice.

HBO has a reputation for making prestige TV shows that set a high bar for the industry. Many of the greatest TV shows of all time are available to watch now on HBO Max, as the streaming service is committed to housing projects that feature top-tier acting, writing, and direction. The company doesn't discriminate when it comes to genre or story type, as every niche of filmmaking has found a home on HBO Max, ensuring the streamer will retain its status as the home of perfect television.

Though Industry is often compared to Succession, the thriller series is completely its own, even if fans of Succession are sure to enjoy it. Set in the high-stakes world of finance, Industry follows a compelling and ruthless group of characters who aren't afraid to betray each other to get ahead in their field. Though Industry season 5 will be the final installment of the show, its impact and detailed exploration of international finance will keep the series in the culture conversation for years to come.

Kate Winslet gives a career-defining performance as Mare Sheehan in the emotional thriller miniseries Mare of Easttown. A series so good that it's easy to rewatch every year and still be moved by the twists of the story, Mare of Easttown follows Mare as she investigates the disappearance of one young woman and the murder of another in her tight-knit community in Pennsylvania. The crimes she investigates draw parallels to her own traumatic past, which Mare must face in order to solve the mysteries that plague her.

Station Eleven was adapted from the novel of the same name by Emily St. John Mandel, and does justice to the author's beautiful narrative about hope and connection in the face of the end of the world. More relevant than ever, Station Eleven chronicles the intersecting lives of several characters, primarily Kirsten (Mackenzie Davis) in the wake of a flu that wipes out most of the population. With elements of science fiction and a nonlinear story structure, the miniseries is unlike anything else on television.

Based on the web series by creator Issa Rae, Awkward Black Girl, Insecure is one of the best comedies from HBO in recent years, largely thanks to the charisma of Rae in the leading role of Issa. Chronicling the highs and lows of her personal, professional, and romantic experiences, Insecure is a classic sitcom in many ways, making it all the more important since so few series at the time focused on the everyday lives of Black women. Hilarious, heartfelt, and full of beautiful friendships, Insecure will appeal to audiences everywhere, not just comedy fans.

There are elements of fantasy and magical realism in The Leftovers, but this is far from the most interesting part of the series. Following a group of characters dealing with the loss and fallout of a global event in which two percent of the population mysteriously disappears without a trace, The Leftovers is a stunning meditation on grief, love, and what it means to have faith. With Justin Theroux, Carrie Coon, and Margaret Qualley prominently featured in the show, The Leftovers might bring tears to your eyes, but it ends on a note of genuine hope.

In one of his best projects to date, The Rehearsal, Nathan Fielder makes the audience laugh, cringe, and gasp in equal measure as he raises the stakes of his experimental docu-series with every episode. Incredibly ambitious, detailed, and never what viewers expect, Fielder has made his name with boundary-pushing work like The Rehearsal. For fans who recently watched his documentary feature You Can See Everything, The Rehearsal will scratch the same itch.

Though there are still, thankfully, more seasons of The Pitt coming soon, the episodes that are out have had overwhelmingly positive critical and audience reception for a reason. With ER veteran Noah Wyle leading the ensemble cast, The Pitt breathes more life into his characters and sprawling hospital staff than any medical show that's come before it. Tackling the very real issues faced by healthcare workers today, The Pitt opens audiences' eyes to the experiences of these individuals through fast-paced, jaw-dropping storytelling.

A key member of The Pitt ER confirms their return for season 3 after the hit HBO Max series was hit with multiple cast changes in development.

Created by, written by, and starring Michaela Coel, I May Destroy You remains one of the best limited series of the 21st century. Fun to watch because of its pace and characters, and unafraid to face difficult themes head-on, I May Destroy You doesn't pull any punches. Following Coel's Arabella​​​​, who uncovers the secrets surrounding her assault, the series handles the sensitive subject matter with grace, but doesn't shy away from the harsh and intense realities of being a survivor. Cathartic and timeless, I May Destroy You will stay with you for a long time.

Revolving around the corruption, brutality, and moments of humanity in Baltimore, Maryland, there was nothing like The Wire when it first premiered in 2002. A sweeping exploration of a city, The Wire isn't just a police drama but a deep investigation into politics, education, and journalism in Baltimore, demonstrating how interconnected these institutions are. Featuring incredible performances from Dominic West, Lance Reddick, Wendell Pierce, and many more, The Wire leaves viewers shocked with every landmark season.

While many HBO shows aired during the era of peak prestige TV in the late 1990s and early 2000s, Succession heralded a new period of impressive filmmaking for the company. Following the treacherous, powerful, and highly influential Roy family and their media conglomerate, Waystar RoyCo, Succession is a withering satire and class commentary, painting a picture of a despicable family you can't stop watching. Though it would've been easy to keep watching Succession for many seasons, the perfectly crafted show ends on a deeply satisfying note.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The Woman Who Said Yes While Shaking Her Head No

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 8, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Early on in my career, I was a research manager working on consumer health products for a pharmaceutical company. Part of my job was to develop ideas for new products by testing them in large quantitative surveys to forecast sales. We showed respondents product descriptions I had painstakingly written and asked how likely they would be to buy it.

We tabulated the results to the decimal point, giving the data an aura of accuracy. But there was one problem. What people said in surveys didn’t reliably predict what they bought in market.

I came across a clue as to why sitting behind a two-way mirror. The focus group moderator was reading a product concept we tested in an earlier survey. She asked: “If this was available, would you buy it?” Without hesitating, a woman across the table clearly said yes, while her head was already shaking no.

The woman herself and the moderator didn’t notice. And we all tallied a yes in the backroom.

But her words just didn’t match her gesture. This doesn’t mean a headshake is the same thing as a lie detector. I wasn’t sure what she might do later. But I knew at that time that her answer was less settled than she made it sound.

From then on, I would note verbal and nonverbal responses, and I kept seeing the same split. A yes would arrive with a wince, eye roll, or furrowed brow. Sometimes they would say deadpan they are excited to buy. I would ask them to write down their reasons. The written words seemed well-reasoned and coherent. Over time, the unspoken reactions helped explain what my self-reported surveys were missing. It’s not like the words were useless. But they weren’t the clear window into behavior that the market research industry was built on.

I also had been assuming that honest people could explain their answers. But I started doubting that, and it changed my career path. The pharmaceutical company offered me a paid executive MBA at Columbia. I asked if I could get a degree in psychology. They said no, so I said no. I left to work at a qualitative research firm conducting focus groups and in-depth interviews. The answers weren’t in my spreadsheets, so I’d rather be in the room with people, so I could see the real story show up before the words.

Years later, I found a different kind of research and an aha moment that helped explain the “say versus do gap” that had undermined my earlier self-reported quantitative forecasts.

Neuroscientist Michael Gazzaniga conducted fascinating studies on patients whose corpus callosum, the fibers connecting the two hemispheres of the brain, had been cut to control severe epilepsy. The surgery sharply limited communication between hemispheres, which allowed him to show patients information to one side of the brain without the other side seeing it.

A patient was shown the word “walk” to their right hemisphere and he got up and began walking and the experimenter asked him why. The left hemisphere, which controls speech, hadn’t seen the instruction, so the honest answer should have been "I don’t know." But instead, he said something completely sincere: "I was thirsty and wanted a drink."

Gazzaniga called this left hemisphere function the interpreter: a process that takes something we just did and creates the most coherent explanation based upon whatever information is available.

The woman behind the glass wasn’t a split-brain patient. Her hemispheres communicated normally. We can’t compare the two research studies literally. But what she did share with split-brain patients was the gap: a fluent verbal response that didn’t match her actions. People with intact brains base their explanations on much more information and they often get it right. But Gazzaniga argues that the interpreter is not just a quirk from surgery but something all our brains do as we attempt to make our experiences feel unified and our actions seem like our own doing. The surgery simply made the interpreter more visible; by cutting off information we would normally use.

You can see this in people with normal brains. Researchers at Lund University in Sweden conducted an experiment that let people in a supermarket taste jam. Shoppers were asked which jam they preferred and then asked to taste it a second time. But the experimenters secretly swapped out their preferred jam with another. Most of the participants didn’t even notice the swap, and when asked to describe why they preferred it, they easily explained why, even though they had just rejected it. Despite their brain being fully intact, their reasons seemed like honest reports when they may have been made up.

Have you ever bought something on a whim? You can probably think of a reason why easily. But here’s a harder question: How do you know that is the real reason? Maybe the answer arrived later based on what you know about the product, the moment you bought it, and the person you believe yourself to be?

Or how about bigger decisions, like the job that felt right, the person you just met that you trusted, or that comment from your loved one that made you snap, only to explain it after as: I was just having a bad day. I’m not saying your explanations are fictitious. But maybe it was the simplest story you could put together on such short notice, while leaving out a deeper feeling of being dismissed by them, or some other worry you felt but hadn’t yet named.

Your reasons are not necessarily lies. But alone they’re incomplete. Sometimes there’s a story, and then there’s the real story. When you close a conversation with a quick confident answer you miss an opportunity to dig deeper for a question that deserves to stay open a little longer.

If you ever find yourself doing something surprising, remember your first explanation is just the start not the verdict. Think about what was happening right before and try to parse out if you were moving away or toward something. We tend to avoid bad feelings rather than try to get what we want. We choose a more expensive bottle to avoid looking cheap, agree with someone to avoid a fight, or put off doing something because we fear what might happen. Those reasons are usually not the first things to come to mind. Make sure to check if your story fits everything, not just the things you noticed.

I never learned what that woman finally did. She said yes, but her body said no. And while both responses came from her, neither told the whole story.

Your explanation may be sincere, but it also might be incomplete.

Gazzaniga, M. S. (1998, July 7). Cognitive neuroscientist Michael Gazzaniga on brain hemispheres and behavior [Interview transcript]. Fresh Air Archive: Interviews with Terry Gross. https://freshairarchive.org/segments/cognitive-neuroscientist-michael-gazzaniga-brain-hemispheres-and-behavior

Gazzaniga, M. S. (2011). Who’s in charge? Free will and the science of the brain. Ecco.

Hall, L., Johansson, P., Tärning, B., Sikström, S., & Deutgen, T. (2010). Magic at the marketplace: Choice blindness for the taste of jam and the smell of tea. Cognition, 117(1), 54–61. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cognition.2010.06.010

Is It True That Air Force One Pilots Earn Around $215K While Big 3 Widebody Captains Clear $480K?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

United States Air Force VC-46A aircraft and the Air Force VC-25B "Bridge" (aka Air Force One) aircraft are military aircraft owned and operated by the United States Air Force and are crewed and piloted by USAF commissioned officers. These are paid as US military officers, and so the Air Force has little flexibility to provide extra pay to its pilots. The base pay is the same as any other US officer serving any branch (including the Coast Guard and Space Force) and doesn't change by responsibility. So an Army officer receives the same base pay as a pilot of the same rank and service.

This is problematic, as it can cost around $10-15 million to train a fast jet pilot. It has left the Air Force exposed to intense competition from commercial airlines that can offer double the total compensation with what is sometimes considered a better work-life balance. One way the Air Force (and the Marines and Navy) have sought to counter this is with retention bonuses, although these are limited, and commercial airlines do the same. Here is what to know about how much commercial widebody pilots are paid and how much Air Force One pilots are paid.

The Air Force does not pay a special "Air Force One salary." The pilots who fly these special Jumbos (and other Air Force aircraft the President flies on) are commissioned officers assigned to the 89th Airlift Wing. As with other US Air Force officers, they are paid according to their military rank and years of service. They do not get a special salary based on the prestige of the aircraft they fly.

Lieutenant Colonels with less than two years of service earn $7,295 per month in base salary, while those with six years of aviation service have a base pay of $9,250 monthly. For Colonels, the base pay is $8,751 for under two years of service and $10,284 for six years. But while six years is the highest number the US Air Force's table goes up to, in practice all Air Force One pilots have a much longer service and earn more.

Lieutenant Colonels typically have 15–17 years of commissioned service, while Colonels typically have 21–24 years of commissioned service. A Lieutenant Colonel flying VC-46s in 2026 can be expected to have a base pay of around $130,000 to $145,000, plus a Basic Allowance for Housing (BAH) of around $30,000 to $45,000, an Aviation Retention Bonus of around $35,000 to $50,000, plus other benefits for a total compensation of between $200,000 and $250,000. A Colonel can expect to earn $235,000 to $285,000 in total compensation.

The US Air Force pilots who fly the VC-46s are typically experienced Lieutenant Colonels (O-5) or Colonels (O-6). This makes them a higher rank than the typical ranks that fly fighter jets on combat missions. Most fighter jet pilots who actually carry out combat missions are First Lieutenants (O-2) and Captains (0-3). At the next rank of Major (0-4), administrative and leadership responsibilities increase, although many majors continue to fly operational and combat missions extensively.

Majors tend to carry out fewer combat missions, although they do extensively perform combat missions as required, often as squadron commanders of a strike package. It is even less common for Lieutenant Colonels and Colonels to fly in combat, although they can if a need arises. In 2026, the F-15E Strike Eagle Weapons Systems Officer that was downed over Iran is reported to have been a Colonel. On average, pilots assigned to the presidential airlift tend to be more senior in rank than pilots in operational fighter squadrons.

As such, Air Force One pilots do tend to earn more than their fighter jet (and transport) counterparts, although it is related to rank and years of service, not the aircraft per se. The 89th Airlift Wing also employs many majors and occasionally captains, although it's unclear if they fly the VC-46s. The wing has a large range of aircraft.

The 89th Airlift Wing (89 AW) is based at Joint Base Andrews, Maryland, and is responsible for operating the USAF's primary executive and special air mission (SAM) fleet. According to its website, it "provides global Special Air Mission airlift, logistics, aerial port, and communications for the President, Vice President, cabinet members and senior leaders to ensure nuclear command, control and communications, continuity of government and continuity of operations."

Its website also listed its aircraft as including two VC-25A (Air Force One) aircraft, four C-32A aircraft (often called Air Force Two), four C-40B Clippers, and eleven C-37A/Bs based on the Gulfstream business jet. This list appears somewhat out of date, as the wing now operates the VC-25B "Bridge" (ex-Qatari Boeing 747-8) Presidential aircraft. Simple Flying has previously reported that the Air Force has quietly purchased an expanded fleet of shadowy Boeing 757s; these are also operated by the 89th Airlift Wing.

89th Airlift Wing fleet (per USAF, others)

The most notable squadron is the Presidential Airlift Group / Presidential Airlift Squadron, which is primarily responsible for the VC-25s. Other notable squadrons include the 1st Airlift Squadron (operating the C-32A and the C-40B) and the 99th Airlift Squadron (operating the C-37 fleet). The wing has a "ready force" of over 1,800 personnel.

While the Air Force is heavily constrained in how much it can pay its pilots, commercial operators are not. Senior widebody captains routinely earn $400,000 to over $520,000 annually. Top widebody captains' hourly rates at all three carriers are around $465 per hour. To some extent, the pay structure in the commercial sector is similar to that in the military.

Commercial pilots are paid based on seniority and years of service with the airline, not so much the type of aircraft they fly. They are also based on their rank, although in the commercial sector, this is limited to captains and first officers. The primary reason why widebody aircraft captains are paid more than narrowbody captains is that pilots bid to fly the widebody aircraft based on their seniority and years of service.

This is somewhat similar to how it is with the prestigious Air Force One fleet. Those pilots are typically paid more than their fighter jet, transport, and other pilot counterparts, but that is due mostly to their seniority. They can only fly the VC-25s as they are very senior pilots. At the rank of Colonel, most pilots in the United States Air Force do not routinely fly military aircraft on operational missions, although there are numerous exceptions.

The Big Three US-based airlines are Delta Air Lines, American Airlines, and United Airlines. Alaska Airlines now operates a limited fleet of Boeing 787 Dreamliners, and Hawaiian Airlines has a fleet of Airbus A330s. Southwest is one of the world's largest airlines, but it exclusively operates the Boeing 737 narrowbody. Of the big three, American has the smallest and simplest widebody fleet made up of 67 Boeing 777s and 70 Boeing 787s.

Delta Air Lines has the next largest widebody fleet, with 57 legacy Boeing 767s being phased down. It also has a fleet of 81 Airbus A330s and 41 Airbus A350s. Delta has placed an order for 30 Boeing 787-10s with options for 30 more, and it has 16 outstanding orders for the A330neo and 39 orders for the A350. American's current outstanding widebody order book is for 19 Boeing 787s.

United Airlines has the largest widebody fleet, and the second largest in the world after Emirates. It operates an all-Boeing fleet of 88 Boeing 787 Dreamliners, 96 Boeing 777s, and 53 aging 767s. United has recently canceled the delayed order for Airbus A350s, meaning its widebody fleet is to stay a Boeing fleet.

It should be noted that there are other considerations when comparing income between the military and commercial sectors, although these can be difficult to simply quantify. For example, pilots in the US Air Force are guaranteed a government-backed lifetime pension of at least 20 years. With American, Delta, and United, traditional pensions are largely gone for new hires, although they contribute around 18% of pay into 401(k)-type plans.

Under the Air Force's retirement system, a pilot can retire after 20 years of service—often in their late 30s or early 40s—and begin drawing an immediate lifetime pension while pursuing a second career. There is no direct equivalent in the commercial airline industry, where pilots generally continue flying until the FAA's mandatory retirement age of 65 while building retirement savings through employer-funded defined-contribution plans.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

This is also a contributing factor to the Air Force's ability to retain pilots and, later, struggle to retain pilots. The military pension provides a strong incentive for pilots to remain in uniform until they reach the 20-year milestone. A pilot can serve 20 years with the Air Force, retire, be employed by a commercial airline, and then receive those additional pension contributions. That said, it's worth noting that the Air Force pension increases if the pilot serves beyond 20 years.

Green Thumb Ended Its String of Profitable Quarters. Is the Stock a Buy?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Green Thumb Industries ended its run of four consecutive profitable quarters.

One big edge that Green Thumb Industries (GTBIF +8.48%) had until recently was that it was one of the few marijuana retailers to turn a profit. On Nov. 4, however, the Chicago-based company reported a third-quarter loss per share of $0.02, the first time since 2025's Q2 that it has lost money.

That's not the end of the world, though. The key is that Green Thumb continues to grow sales. It reported Q3 revenue of $324.4 million, up 11.3%, year over year, showing growing demand for its consumer brand portfolio.

Here are three reasons to buy Green Thumb Industries.

Green Thumb maintains a cash-generative operating profile and balance-sheet discipline. Operational cash flow remains solid at $58 million in Q3, following $76 million in Q1 and $29 million in Q2, enabling management to aggressively return capital to shareholders.

Over the first nine months of 2026, Green Thumb repurchased 13.4 million shares for $77.7 million, reducing outstanding share count from 206.6 million down to 197.5 million, and recently authorized a new $50 million share buyback program.

The primary reason for the Q3 net loss is the company's continued expansion. It opened six RISE dispensaries in the quarter: Port Charlotte and Port St. Lucie in Florida; Sparks in Nevada; and Hanover, West Mifflin, and Allentown in Pennsylvania.

The company is positioned to capture future growth as several major key states transition toward adult-use legalization or expanded access. Green Thumb holds a vertically integrated license in Virginia, where recreational retail launches in July 2027, and a conditional license under Texas' Compassionate Use Program, and continues to expand retail density in states such as New Jersey, Ohio, and Pennsylvania. Together, these expansion markets provide clear multiyear revenue runways.

Green Thumb is No. 2 in quarterly revenue among the top U.S. cannabis retailers, behind Curaleaf, which recently reported $353 million in Q3. Compared to Curaleaf and Trulieve, Green Thumb is well priced, with a price-to-sales ratio of 1.3. It also has a lower debt-to-earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) level than Curaleaf or Trulieve because it carries less debt.

In Q3, the company reported cash of $316 million and total debt of $277.3 million. Green Thumb is already benefiting from the rescheduling of cannabis to a Schedule III substance. It spent $28.1 million on income taxes in the quarter, nearly half the $49.1 million it spent in the same period a year ago.

Its operating cash flow was $58 million in the quarter, down from $74.1 million in the same quarter in 2025, but that's still superior to the $53 million Trulieve reported in Q2 and the $22 million that Curaleaf reported in the third quarter.

James Halley has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool recommends Green Thumb Industries. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Cineplex Boards Sanfic Title ‘La Residencia’ From ‘Algo Azul’ Director Mariel García Spooner

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Latin American distributor has acquired North and South American sales rights to the title, now in post-production, at the Santiago Film Festival

Latin American distributor Cineplex has picked up both North and South American sales rights to “La Residencia,” Mariel Garcia Spooner’s follow up to hit “Algo Azul.”

“La Resdemcia” has just participated in the Sanfic Industria‘s WIP Iberoamericana section, a competition of films from Latin America, Spain and Portugal screened in post-production at the industry arm of Santiago de Chile’s Sanfic festival. Related Stories Cristian Mungiu's Palme d'Or Winner 'Fjord' Takes Jakarta World Cinema Audience Award as Short Film Winners Land Debut Feature Funding '3 Body Problem' Director Derek Tsang Boards DEA True-Crime Series 'Group 41' From Zhang Yimou’s 'Shadow' Producer Jonah Greenberg (EXCLUSIVE)

Directed by Panamanian filmmaker García Spooner, the dramedy follows three women friends in their mid-sixties who join forces in order to jointly face their money troubles and reinvent their future by creating a social club for seniors.

Said Cineplex managing partner Diana Dudek, who works alongside co-founder-general manager Elba McAllister: “We have known Mariel for several years and have been working with her since her first film, ‘Algo Azul.’ We like her way of telling entertaining, relatable stories with a deeper underlying message.”

“With ‘La Residencia,’ from the moment we read the screenplay, we immediately connected with the story of these three friends who, at a more mature stage of their lives, decide to reinvent themselves and move forward together.”

The dramedy’s development trajectory includes it participation at Málaga WIP, after which it won the Latino Film Institute Work in Progress finishing fund at Laliff 2026, presented in collaboration with Amazon MGM Studios. In addition, Barcelona-based Only Postproduction boarded the project to support its color grading, following a connection established during Mafiz.

It stars Colombian actresses Xilena Aycardi and Marcela Carvajal, alongside Panamanian cast members Diana Abouganem, Massiel Mas, Diego de Obaldía, Miguel Melfi, Beatriz García and Gregory Manrique.

Said García Spooner: “With ‘La Residencia,’ I tell the story of Cecilia, Rocío and Martina from a close and human perspective. They are three friends who begin to question what they want for their future and how to remain independent while everything around them is changing. The film explores friendship, the fear of growing older and above all, the possibility of starting over without losing who you are.”

Her Panama-based Hello October company produced the dramedy. Among its credits is Garcia Spooner’s debut feature “Algo Azul,” which was theatrically released in 11 territories and played on HBO Max and Amazon’s Prime Video. It also produced “Tumbadores,“ which is on Prime Video.

Now picture locked, “La Residencia” is in its final stages of post-production ahead of its festival launch.

“The film has the potential to resonate with international audiences. We are delighted to be working with Mariel again, and we are confident that we will find the right partners to bring this film to audiences across different international markets,” said Dudek.

Cineplex also picked up Colombian and Latin American rights to another Sanfic WIP title, “The Cry of Serankwa” by Yull Nuñez.

Cineplex’s other recent acquisitions include the documentary “Yuma” and Klych López’s “Irreparable,” which world premieres at the Tallinn Black Nights film fest and has its Latin American premiere at Argentina’s Mar del Plata fest, said McAllister. Starring Marcela Valencia and Marcela Mar, it follows two estranged sisters whose reunion triggers long-harbored resentments.

A Look Inside WELL/BEINGS’s ‘Bound by Nature’ Gala

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On November 6, cultural leaders, philanthropists, and conservation advocates braved the rain in New York City for a worthy cause. That evening, WELL/BEINGS hosted its annual benefit dinner at Hearst Tower, bringing guests together to support conservation efforts in the most vital and vulnerable ecosystems across the Caribbean and the Bolivian Amazon.

The event, titled “Bound by Nature” this year, was hosted by actor, entrepreneur, and philanthropist Oliver Trevena, and presented by the nonprofit’s co-founders Amanda Hearst Rønning and Breanna Schultz. Throughout the night, attendees enjoyed live music, conservation storytelling, an exclusive auction of fine art, jewelry, and luxury experiences, as well as a specially curated culinary experience by celebrity chef Eduardo Garcia.

As nods to the marine and terrestrial environments, the sea of guests wore shades of blue and green, respectively.

The benefit sought to raise funds toward more than $800,000 in programmatic commitments WELL/BEINGS has made to 15 conservation partners for 2026 and 2027. The selected organizations, which include local and underserved groups, are working to protect wildlife, restore critical habitats, and strengthen communities in targeted areas.

During her opening remarks, Hearst Rønning underscored the importance of investing in the people closest to the ecosystems in need of protection: “That is why we support and amplify local leaders,” she said. “They know what needs to be done. Too often, what stands between them and doing it is support and funding.”

Schultz echoed that sentiment, reminding attendees of the lasting impact of their contributions: “Our partners fight every day to protect what we love. Tonight, we can give them the resources to keep going…what we save now is what the next generation will inherit.”

Since its founding, WELL/BEINGS has invested more than $3 million in conservation. The organization also operates under a 100 percent donation model, with its co-founders and Board of Directors separately covering core operating expenses so that public donations go directly toward the mission at hand.

The evening also marked the launch of the Caribbean Bioregional Hub, a new initiative connecting nine partner organizations across the region to strengthen collaboration and advance coordinated conservation efforts. The sky is the limit!

Born and raised in New York City to Panamanian parents, she’ll take book recs in English, Spanish (and soon, German). Her interests include music, fashion, beauty, film, earth science, and the study of love. On her time off, you can find her with loved ones at an odd-hour movie showtime, baking, or sitting in silence near a high-fidelity sound system.

Xabi Alonso knows he has at least one Chelsea dependable

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Chelsea, featuring team news and expert analysis from Dom Smith

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

When Emmanuel Emegha came on in the 87th minute to join Danny Welbeck up front, the only surprising thing, as Chelsea got desperate in their bid to save themselves at the last at Sunderland, was that it was Jordan Henderson to make way.

Henderson was unlikely to provide the goal threat Chelsea needed in those dying embers of Sunday’s eventual 1-0 defeat, but he had increasingly been the free man and the right passing option for whoever in yellow and black had the ball at their feet as the match had gone on.

A defeat at the Stadium of Light to the side who had begun the day at the bottom of the Premier League table was a grim result for Xabi Alonso’s team to head into the international break with, especially given it was reminiscent of the 3-0 loss at Brentford ahead of the last hiatus.

Chelsea were devoid of ideas and lacking any semblance of the sort of control Alonso has publicly called for from his players.

And yet whatever it is about his team that is failing to give him that control and dominance right now, the problem is to be found elsewhere than where Henderson is going about his business.

Though they won last weekend against Manchester United without him, his return at Sunderland following illness was a welcome one. A midfield of Moises Caicedo plus either Romeo Lavia or Henderson or both, as was deployed on Sunday, is just about the only thing functioning as it should be for Chelsea right now.

For Henderson personally, it was a full-circle afternoon in the North East. Henderson is Sunderland born and bred and his appearance was significant. It was for Sunderland, against Chelsea, that 18 years and seven days earlier he had made his senior debut. Now came the 700th appearance, donning the colours of the visiting team.

The visitors faltered. With a controversial penalty given against them and Cole Palmer contentiously not awarded one of his own, Palmer and Morgan Rogers both had off-days, Levi Colwill was deemed expendable enough to be substituted at half-time, and none of Alonso’s substitutions could force the issue and muster an equaliser.

Henderson was sensible in possession when others around him were not

Henderson, though, was sensible in possession when others around him were not. He kept the ball moving, with one-touch passes into feet, relieving the pressure Chelsea were under in one of English football’s more hostile grounds.

Alonso won all five games against Sunderland as a Liverpool player and scored the winner in one of them, yet no such Midas touch in Chelsea colours from the technical area this time. His Chelsea gave Sunderland their first win in eight Premier League matches.

Winning blockbuster battles against the so-called ‘big six’ is important, but Premier League titles are won and European places earned by finding consistency, by winning home and away against the Sunderlands, Hulls and Fulhams of the Premier League.

Chelsea are yet to reach that point, as Henderson — who spent years in Liverpool teams that could and did — will be all too aware. The 36-year-old is not the problem. He actually represents what there is that’s working.

Here's the Average Woman's Social Security Benefit by Age

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Women's average Social Security benefit runs about $410 less monthly than men's, a gap rooted in earnings history and workforce participation.

Social Security benefits for women average less than those for men across every age group. This report lays out the specific dollar amounts as of December 2025, the most recent data available, to show the current size of that gap.

The average benefit among women is $1,872.43 per month, according to the Social Security Administration (SSA). That figure is $409.85 lower than the $2,282.28 average monthly benefit for men.

The table below shows the average monthly payments for retirees aged 62 to 70. These figures reflect the amounts as of December 2025 and illustrate the consistent difference in checks between men and women.

The SSA bases your benefit on an average of your wages across your highest-earning 35 years. It adjusts those earlier wages for inflation and runs the results through the primary insurance amount (PIA) formula. The persistent gap in benefit amounts between men and women reflects historical differences in lifetime earnings and fewer years spent in the paid workforce.

These payments are designed to replace only about 40% of your pre-retirement income, which is well short of what most retirees need to cover their costs.

Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later. Workers who claim at 62 accept a reduction in their monthly check, while those who wait until 70 earn delayed retirement credits worth 8% per year.

While women receive lower average monthly checks, those benefits typically have to stretch over a longer period. A woman who is 65 today is expected to live about 21 more years, compared to about 18 more years for a man, according to the SSA. This means the average woman needs her retirement income to last roughly three years longer than the average man.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027 to help these checks keep pace with inflation. For a 70-year-old woman receiving the average $2,024.08 benefit, that adjustment adds about $73 per month to that figure. The dollar gap is widest at the oldest ages in the table, and spousal and survivor benefits are part of the picture for many women.

The above information focuses on benefits for retired workers, but many women claim spousal or survivor benefits based on their partner's work records instead. This could give them more money than they'd qualify for on their own.

Spousal benefits are the benefits you're eligible for on your partner's work record while they're still alive. They're worth up to one-half of the amount your partner qualifies for at their FRA. You will only be able to receive this amount once your spouse is claiming their retirement benefits.

Survivor benefits are the benefits you're eligible for on your partner's work record after they pass away. These are worth up to 100% of the amount your spouse was eligible for or receiving at the time of their death. You will only get this amount if it's larger than your own retirement benefit.

Contact the Social Security Administration to learn more about whether you qualify for these benefits and how much they could be worth to you.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

American Airlines Updates Vouchers For Stranded Passengers—But Tells Employees There May Be Fewer Hotel Choices

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

American Airlines is updating how stranded passengers get hotel rooms, meal credits and rides during flight disruptions. An internal November memo I reviewed lays out automatic offers for eligible overnight delays and a new way for agents to arrange Uber rides without making passengers download the app. It also warns that employees may see fewer hotels to choose from. Getting passengers out of the customer service line and into a room sooner is great for getting sleep. On a recent American Airlines flight from New York to Austin that was cancelled after a long delay I didn’t even bother getting into the long line at the gate for a room voucher, I just booked something myself nad went on my way. What American Is Changing The update covers American’s Hotel, Meal and Transportation system, which employees call HMT. October 26: standalone meal credits. Agents can issue food and beverage credits for individual reservations affected by qualifying delays, cancellations and missed connections. The system uses flight information to evaluate eligibility and tells agents what qualifies. October 27: automatic offers for eligible overnight delays. The system sends hotel offers and applicable food and beverage credits when its eligibility and automation criteria are met, reducing the need for an employee to initiate the offer. Planned Uber Central rollout. At participating airports, authorized agents would arrange a ride on the customer’s behalf through the same system. The passenger receives a text message or WhatsApp link to start the ride, without downloading Uber. This should now be available but I haven’t seen passenger reports of the broadly available rollout yet (though customers reported it in limited release previously). Hotel inventory changes, date undetermined. American is changing the hotel inventory feed and selection process. Employees are told they may see fewer hotel choices. Automatic Vouchers Already Exist American has been working on this for years. I covered the electronic hotel and meal system in 2019, when agents still had to qualify customers and trigger offers. American was already describing automatic hotel, meal and transportation vouchers in 2025. This spring, they added meal issuance for an entire flight at once. In June, automatic meal vouchers expanded beyond elite members to all AAdvantage members. American’s January app changes already brought disruption assistance together: Their published customer service plan promises, upon request, an approved hotel for an overnight disruption they cause away from your city of residence, transportation there and back, and a food and beverage credit after a qualifying delay of at least three hours. The standard meal voucher I’ve been covering is $12. American recently renamed it a “food/beverage credit” because calling it a meal voucher led passengers to expect it to pay for a meal. How The Uber Change Works American already offers Uber vouchers. The change lets an agent arrange the ride for the passenger. Uber Central supports rides paid for by the business, with trip details sent to the customer. However, American’s instructions still put the hotel shuttle first, followed by an Uber voucher. Uber Central is for exceptions when those options don’t meet the customer’s needs. One passenger reported being at Washington National when agents refused Uber vouchers because a hotel had a shuttle even though it wasn’t running at 1 a.m. Fewer Hotel Choices Isn’t Great The memo’s most concerning sentence is: Frontline teams may see fewer hotel choices displayed than they are accustomed to seeing. Being able to choose a hotel matters. I’ve covered passenger complaints about unsuitable rooms and missing shuttles. Here’s a passenger who said the hotel floor was so dirty they spread sheets over it. If American can’t provide an approved hotel voucher, its customer service plan promises reimbursement of reasonable hotel costs. Its conditions of carriage also say reimbursement isn’t guaranteed when you book directly without written authorization. Credit card trip protections may reimburse required hotel overnights as well. ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Powers $31M in Historic Box Office Launch Against Subdued ‘Cat in the Hat’

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The sequel to 2023's 'Godzilla Minus One' made noise as 'Ramayana' and 'Wild Horse Nine' also debuted.

Godzilla Minus Zero is king of the box office in its opening weekend as the sequel feature tallies the biggest launch in its distributor’s history with nearly three times the debut of predecessor Godzilla Minus One.

Hitting 3,254 North American theaters from GKids and Toho, Godzilla Minus Zero conquered $31.6 million domestically for the weekend. This is being touted as the highest-grossing live-action foreign-language opening at the box office since 2004’s Hero launched with $17.8 million. (It’s behind the debut of last year’s animated Demon Slayer: Infinity Castle with $70.6 million, while 2004’s The Passion of the Christ and its massive $83.8 million opening is not included in the category as an American-produced project.) Godzilla Minus Zero notches the highest-grossing opening weekend for a GKids release, topping the $13 million for 2023’s Oscar-winning The Boy and the Heron. Related Stories TV Druski Embodies Elizabeth Holmes in 'You Can See Everything' Spoof on 'SNL' Movies Danny McBride's 'G.I. Joe' Movie Lands 2028 Release From Paramount

Godzilla Minus Zero added $31.4 million internationally, including $17 million in Japan. Filmmaker Takashi Yamazaki‘s sequel to 2023’s Oscar-winning Godzilla Minus One earned an A CinemaScore from audiences and performed well above the distributor’s projected opening in the mid-$20 millions.

Ryunosuke Kamiki, Minami Hamabe and Min Tanaka star in the new movie that carries a reported budget in the mid-teens. It centers on postwar Japan dealing with attacks from the three-headed Ghidorah and the possibility of Godzilla’s return. Godzilla Minus Zero holds an impressive 90 percent critics approval on Rotten Tomatoes, along with a 96 from audiences, with The Hollywood Reporter chief film critic David Rooney calling it a “thrilling jolt of marauding monster action.”

The sequel is making a bigger footprint than Godzilla Minus One, which opened in December 2023 with $11.4 million domestically and reached $57.1 million for the run, adding $56.9 million internationally. The film rode its massive breakout success to become Japan’s first feature to win the best visual effects Oscar.

Overall, the weekend showed an increase of around 11 percent over last weekend, with the studios avoiding new releases for the Halloween frame, and a roughly 40 percent uptick from the equivalent 2025 weekend that saw the debut of 20 Century’s Predator: Badlands, according to FranchiseRe. This October had trouble establishing hits that can continue to play deep into the fall.

Warner Bros.’ The Cat in the Hat grossed a sleepy $20.5 million domestically from 4,003 North American locations, on par with the studio’s projection. The two previous animated Dr. Seuss adaptations had far higher debuts, with Universal’s 2018 The Grinch swiping $67.5 million in its opening, while that studio’s 2012 launch of The Lorax claimed $70.2 million. The studio hopes for word of mouth to help the $92 million-budgeted The Cat in the Hat play through the Thanksgiving holiday and into December after earning an A CinemaScore, with competition on the horizon from Disney’s Hexed on Nov. 25.

Dr. Seuss films historically haven’t made a big splash overseas, and this one has staggered its international release, with around half of the territories set for later debuts throughout the remainder of the year. The Cat in the Hat added $10 million internationally for the frame.

Directors Erica Rivinoja and Alessandro Carloni’s animated adaptation of Dr. Seuss’ classic 1957 children’s book centers on the titular feline being given a final chance to prove his worth by helping a pair of siblings. Bill Hader leads the voice cast that also includes Matt Berry, Quinta Brunson and Xochitl Gomez. The movie marks Warner Bros. Pictures Animation’s first title since Bill Damaschke took over as president and chief creative officer in 2023.

Critics have been a bit aloof on the film, as it holds a 46 percent Rotten Tomatoes score from reviewers and an 87 percent score from audiences, with THR critic Frank Scheck calling it “more taxing than enjoyable.” It’s faring better critically than Universal’s 2003 live-action The Cat in the Hat starring Mike Myers, which holds an 11 percent score on Rotten Tomatoes. That movie opened to $38.3 million domestically en route to a North American cume of $101 million against a $109 million budget.

Sony Pictures released Prime Focus Studios’ Ramayana: Rise of a Legend in 1,433 North American locations, with the film grossing roughly $8.6 million for the weekend and $12.1 million for the four days. It added $32 million in India and an additional $11.5 million from 37 overseas markets. Boasting the widest domestic launch ever for an Indian movie, director Nitesh Tiwari’s religious epic focuses on the life of Prince Rama, with the two-film project’s budget reported at $420 million and beyond. It holds a 76 score from critics on Rotten Tomatoes, along with an 89 from audiences.

Also debuting in the top five is Angel Studios’ Drummer Boy, with directors Ben and Joel David Smallbone’s historical drama grossing a strong $9.3 million from 2,850 North American locations. Earning an A CinemaScore, the faith-based musical focuses on an unlikely bond during the American Revolution and stars Joel David Smallbone in the title role. On Rotten Tomatoes, it has a 50 critics score and a 94 from audiences. Angel was uniquely positioned to open the film in a major way after having established itself with the faith-minded audience.

“It has been eight long years of failure and triumph to bring Drummer Boy to the screen,” Joel David Smallbone said in a statement to celebrate the opening. “So as you’d imagine, I find myself deeply impacted by the fact that this personal story has found its way into hundreds of thousands of your hearts.”

Academy Awards hopeful Wild Horse Nine opened in 1,100 North American theaters with an estimated frame of $6 million. John Malkovich, Sam Rockwell and Steve Buscemi lead writer-director Martin McDonagh’s comedic thriller about two CIA operatives who travel from Santiago, Chile, to Easter Island in 1973. The Searchlight Pictures release holds a 94 critics score on Rotten Tomatoes and 91 from audiences, with Rooney’s review describing it as “uniquely affecting.”

Likely rounding out the top 10 is Black Bear’s Wicker, which declined just 23 percent in its third weekend with $3.3 million for a domestic cume of $19.8 million, along with an additional $9.1 million internationally. Olivia Colman and Alexander Skarsgard lead directors Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer’s romantic fantasy that continues to build on enthusiastic word of mouth, holding a 94 critics score on Rotten Tomatoes and 92 from audiences.

Expected to hit just outside the top 10 is Burns & Co and Fathom Entertainment’s Jimmy, with the Jimmy Stewart biopic opening to an estimated $2 million from 1,470 North American theaters. Scheck’s review calls star KJ Apa “top-notch” but deems director Aaron Burns’ film “heartfelt to a fault.”

Another Oscar hopeful launched in platform release as A24 debuted Club Kid on six screens, with the Cannes standout grossing $390,583 for an average of $65,097 per screen. Making his feature directorial debut, Jordan Firstman also stars as a party promoter who learns he has a 10-year-old son. Set to expand into Thanksgiving, it boasts a sterling 100 score from reviewers on Rotten Tomatoes, with THR critic Richard Lawson heralding it as “winning and clever.”

Amazon MGM Studios releases I Play Rocky in seven locations, and it grossed around $345,000 for a per-screen average of $49,200. Anthony Ippolito stars as Sylvester Stallone in director Peter Farrelly’s making-of story for the 1976 classic Rocky. In his review, Rooney wrote that the movie — which holds an 88 Rotten Tomatoes score from critics — creates “a wave of warm nostalgia.”

Among holdovers, A24’s much-discussed Elizabeth Holmes documentary You Can See Everything looks to pull in around $8.8 million for the weekend as it jostles with Ramayana and Drummer Boy to round out the top five. The domestic cume for directors Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s feature stands at $79.7 million.

Next weekend brings the Johnny Depp studio comeback vehicle Ebenezer, along with How to Rob a Bank and Paper Tiger.

Tom Hardy’s Epic 6-Season Crime Series Is a Near-Perfect Netflix Binge

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

From HBO’s The Sopranos to Paramount+’s MobLand, dysfunctional mob families make for great television — but no other group of criminals is quite as cunning, conniving, and calculating as the Shelby family from Netflix’s Peaky Blinders. Starring Oscar-winning Cillian Murphy as the notorious Shelby ringleader Thomas “Tommy” Shelby, Peaky Blinders takes viewers back to Small Heath, Birmingham, in 1919, the town that would witness the Shelbys’ rise, peak, and eventual downfall.

Violence and bloodshed are expected in the series, but Peaky Blinders is just as much a family drama as it is a crime epic. Blood may be thicker than water, but Tommy is a businessman first and a family man second. Across six seasons, Peaky Blinders delivers no shortage of shocking plot twists to keep audiences hooked, made all the more surprising by the fact that many come from the people closest to Tommy.

Inspired by the real-life street gang, Peaky Blinders follows Tommy, fresh out of serving in World War I, as he returns to Small Heath with plans to turn his family’s small horse-betting operation into a legitimate business. His opportunity comes when his men stumble upon a crate of stolen military weapons, setting his bigger plans for the Peaky Blinders into motion. From blackmailing police authorities and taking territory from rival gangs to expanding into bigger cities, each season sees Tommy taking another step toward legitimizing Shelby Company Limited.

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

As Tommy’s ambitions grow, so do the risks. Every season also follows the seemingly untouchable Tommy pushing his luck further, making increasingly questionable decisions to keep his empire growing. He doesn’t even hesitate to use his own family members as collateral, insisting that it’s all for a greater purpose. That's part of the appeal of Peaky Blinders: watching just how long Tommy can get away with his schemes before his luck finally runs out. When a man who has spent his entire life building an empire finds himself with nothing left, the question becomes whether he can rise from the ashes or if this is finally where his story ends.

Peaky Blinders has no shortage of seasoned actors bringing its characters to life. Besides Murphy’s cutthroat performance as the morally ambiguous and frighteningly intelligent Tommy, the rest of the Shelby family are just as gutsy as their leader, with some arguably even more so. From Paul Anderson as Tommy’s violent but troubled older brother, Arthur Shelby, to Helen McCrory as the wiser and more level-headed Aunt Polly Gray, each family member has a distinct personality that makes them fascinating to watch. Put them all in the same room, though, and their explosive chemistry makes audiences wonder how they haven't killed each other yet.

The series also boasts an impressive roster of recurring characters, some of whom become regular fixtures in Tommy's life while others only appear for a season or two. Even with limited screen time, these roles attract plenty of talent. Anya Taylor-Joy, Stephen Graham, and Adrien Brody are just a few of the big names who join the cast in later seasons. Then there's Tom Hardy as Alfie Solomons, the leader of a London-based Jewish gang. Appearing across five seasons, Hardy makes for one of Tommy's most entertaining counterparts, with their unpredictable alliance and rivalry providing some of the series' best moments.

Pierce Brosnan and Helen Mirren also star.

The original Peaky Blinders series ended in 2022, seemingly sealing Tommy’s fate after six seasons of chasing one impossible high after another. However, the story didn't end there. In March 2026, creator Steven Knight released a feature-film continuation, Peaky Blinders: The Immortal Man, set years after the original show's finale. Following its premiere, Netflix doubled down on the franchise by announcing a two-part sequel series focusing on Tommy's sons, Duke (Jamie Bell) and Charles Shelby (Charlie Heaton). The cast also includes Jessica Brown Findlay, Lashana Lynch, and Lucy Karczewski.

Set in 1953, the sequel follows the next generation of Shelbys as Birmingham rebuilds itself after World War II. A massive reconstruction project creates new opportunities, but everyone wants a piece of it. At the center of the action is Duke (Bell), now grown up and even more ruthless than when audiences last saw him in The Immortal Man, where Barry Keoghan played the character. Meanwhile, his half-brother Charles (Heaton) has cut ties with the Shelby family and the Peaky Blinders. But once a Shelby, always a Shelby. With a family that endures for all the wrong reasons, Peaky Blinders is a near-perfect binge for anyone who enjoys watching a powerful crime family rise to the top, only to slowly fall apart from within. If you're caught up on MobLand and you're tired of rewatching The Sopranos, it's time to take a journey to Birmingham next.

A gangster family epic set in 1900s England, centering on a gang who sew razor blades in the peaks of their caps, and their fierce boss Tommy Shelby.

Big-six Brighton? Table since Hurzeler took over illustrates sustained Seagulls success

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brighton have made a flying start to the season and the table since they appointed Fabian Hurzeler illustrates that their fine form is no flash in the pan.

The Seagulls sit second in the Premier League table, the closest challengers to Arsenal after the first 10 games.

Brighton have scored more goals than anyone else this season, and only the Gunners have conceded fewer.

Hurzeler’s success, though, goes back far beyond the start of the current season.

The German was appointed in the summer of 2024. Then a relative unknown and just 31 years old, Hurzeler replaced Roberto De Zerbi, who had led Brighton to their highest-ever finish – sixth – in 2022-23 but left the Italian left the AmEx after the Seagulls finished 11th in 2023-24.

This is the overall Premier League table since Hurzeler took charge in June 2024…

Under Hurzeler’s management, through two full seasons and the start of the current campaign, Brighton are sixth – equal to their highest-ever position.

The Seagulls have achieved this consistency all the while making a net profit in the transfer market over the last five years.

The only season in which they spent more than they made was Hurzeler’s first, when they invested £191million.

Even that spend, though, could yet yield a hefty profit. According to Transfermarkt, the players bought in that period who are still at the club this term have seen their collective value rise by £69million since their Brighton debuts.

Last season, Brighton made £56million in the transfer market and this summer they turned a profit of almost £11million before leading the pursuit of Arsenal and City who, between them in the recent window, had a net spend of more than £240million.

No clear winner in New Zealand election, as leaders prepare for weeks of talks

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

New Zealand Prime Minister and National Party leader, Christopher Luxon addresses party faithful in Auckland, New Zealand, Saturday, Nov.7, 2026. Alex Robertson/AP hide caption

WELLINGTON, New Zealand — New Zealanders will wait for weeks to discover who will lead the country after Saturday's election failed to deliver a decisive win for the right or the left, leaving fraught negotiations for a wafer-thin governing majority the only apparent path to victory for either side.

The tightest vote in years delivered a stinging rebuke to the major parties which have long dominated New Zealand politics, Christopher Luxon's center-right National party and center-left Labour led by Chris Hipkins, as voters threw more support behind minor parties on the ideological flanks than before. On Saturday's numbers, minor parties would enter negotiations for a governing deal with leverage to extract deeper policy concessions from a weakened National or Labour.

The near-deadlock was partly produced because voters, apparently weary of New Zealand's economic woes and apathetic about both Luxon and Hipkins, chose to propel a new entrant group to Parliament for the first time in years. Opportunity, led by 38-year-old business consultant and political newcomer Qiulae Wong, is unaligned with either bloc and Wong has said she could work with either side to form a government.

Wong's pledges of evidence-based centrism and an end to partisan bickering had on Saturday's results secured seven seats for Opportunity — enough to frustrate both left and right in a simple path to power. It was a staggering ascension for a party that made three failed attempts at Parliament before Wong became leader less than a year ago.

Other winners were harder to identify and on Sunday, "who won the election 2026" was the top trending Google search in New Zealand. So unclear was the result that parties won't begin negotiations to form a government until final tallies are released on Nov. 27, confirming the exact number of seats they've won.

With 2.8 million ballots counted on election night, the conservative incumbent government led by Luxon's National party appeared close to the slimmest possible return to power — by just one seat. However, Luxon didn't claim victory, instead striking a note of cautious optimism that he could negotiate a second term in power.

Speaking to reporters Sunday, Luxon blamed public dissatisfaction on global shocks which have buffeted New Zealand's trade-based economy, noting that other incumbent governments worldwide have faced voters' rancor over living costs.

Yet his opponent Hipkins, who was turfed from office in 2023 largely over discontent about the same economic issues, didn't appear to benefit on Saturday. The left-wing bloc appeared to fall short of a majority but was still hopeful it could triumph — though that seemed a more remote prospect.

Electoral officials were due on Sunday to begin counting nearly 400,000 so-called special votes, including from New Zealanders who live abroad, a process expected to take three weeks.

Those ballots have historically granted an additional seat or two in Parliament to left-wing parties, which could snatch the current razor-thin majority from the right, although such an outcome would likely leave the left struggling to form a majority too. If a government can't be formed, New Zealand could face a fresh vote.

Labour Party leader Chris Hipkins addresses party faithful in Upper Hutt, New Zealand, Saturday, Nov. 7, 2026. Mark Tantrum/AP hide caption

"I think everyone's over politics so let's all have a bit of a break for the next three weeks," Luxon told reporters on Sunday. He will lead a caretaker government until an outcome is reached.

Luxon suggested that National had a viable path to power and boasted that it had secured more seats in Parliament than any other group. However, Saturday night was bleak for his party.

Several of National's most senior lawmakers would exit politics on results so far, including the ministers of finance and defense, the speaker of parliament, and the attorney general. With the party's traditional governing partners winning increased support, Luxon faces a more strident list of policy demands to secure their backing.

Libertarian ACT won 13 seats and populist New Zealand First won 12. Both are led by canny veteran politicians, and detractors of Luxon, a former business executive who entered politics in 2020, said his dealings with them in the three years since he took office had highlighted his political inexperience.

The Opportunity Party leader Qiulae Wong poses for a portrait outside Parliament in Wellington, New Zealand, Friday, Oct. 30, 2026. Charlotte Graham-McLay/AP hide caption

New Zealanders don't vote for prime ministers. Instead they cast two votes, one for their preferred party and another for the candidate they want as their local representative.

The country has one house of Parliament and no states, so the central government holds ultimate sway over New Zealand's direction. Governments in the South Pacific nation of 5.6 million people are elected every three years.

National won just 29.21% of the preferred party vote as of Sunday, a collapse of its previous result, and secured its 42 seats entirely by winning local constituency races. Luxon, who unusually faced two separateconfidence votes among his own lawmakers this year, further slumped in popularity as leader close to election day.

However, Labour also won just 29.36% of the party vote, a result that appeared to suggest a second election defeat for career politician Hipkins. Despite winning about the same party vote percentage as National, Labour has secured only 36 seats on numbers so far because they won fewer local races.

Minor parties on the left saw bolstered support. The environmentally-focused Greens won 16 seats and Indigenous rights group Te Pāti Māori secured five.

The total number of seats in New Zealand's Parliament changes due to quirks in how seat allocations are calculated. There would currently be 131 lawmakers elected, with 66 seats required for a majority.

Luxon's conservative bloc would currently hold 67, a razor-thin margin likely to discomfit a leader managing a three-way government with two emboldened minor parties. However, the left bloc held only 57 seats before the historically left-leaning special votes were counted.

Even if the final tally boosted the left bloc's seats slightly, Hipkins would apparently need support from newcomer Wong to govern.

Before the election, Luxon repeatedly ruled out negotiating with Wong. He sidestepped questions on Sunday about whether his vanishingly slim lead had changed his mind.

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

‘On par with what the DoD pursued during the Cold War’: Pentagon’s war stockpile plan hits a minerals wall

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Pentagon wants to stockpile enough critical minerals, fuel, and munitions on American soil to keep fighting a major war even if cyberattacks and sabotage knock out factories and power grids at home. One defense expert says the scale of the planned effort is “on par with what the DoD pursued during the Cold War.”

The minerals may be the hardest part. The U.S. produces less than 1% of the most common type of rare-earth magnet it uses, and China, which dominates the processing of many critical minerals, has kept American companies short even in peacetime.

The initiative, known as FORTRESS America, short for Federal Operations for Resilience, Troop Resources, Energy, Supply, and Survivability, was formally established on Sept. 30. It is designed to keep U.S. forces operating if a conflict disrupts domestic power grids and manufacturing supply chains, and it is the same program behind the Pentagon’s push for major bases to plan for 90 days without the grid.

Stockpiling materials isn’t unusual for the U.S. military, Bryan Clark, a senior fellow at the Hudson Institute, told Fortune. What distinguishes FORTRESS is its planned scale and its shift toward domestic reserves rather than overseas stockpiles.

During the Cold War, the Pentagon stored as much or more war matériel overseas, near anticipated battlefields in Eastern Europe or Northeast Asia, Clark said. FORTRESS instead focuses on protecting supplies inside the United States, reflecting growing concern that American factories could themselves become targets.

China could disrupt U.S. industrial production through cyber operations, drones, electromagnetic warfare, or attacks carried out by people inside the country, Clark warned. U.S. agencies have already warned that Chinese state-sponsored hackers had positioned themselves inside American critical infrastructure.

That concern has a recent precedent in Europe. On Dec. 29, 2025, cyberattacks hit about 30 renewable energy sites, a manufacturing company, and a heat and power plant serving nearly 500,000 customers in Poland. Polish officials linked the attacks to Russia’s Federal Security Service and said the attackers tried to destroy industrial systems, but the country avoided a major outage.

Building stockpiles on the scale Clark described runs into a stark industrial reality: America’s defense industry remains deeply reliant on foreign supply chains for its raw materials.

In 2025, the U.S. imported about $60 billion worth of critical minerals other than rare earths, including nickel, lithium, cobalt, graphite, and tungsten, according to the Peterson Institute for International Economics.

The gap is most acute in rare-earth magnets. U.S. demand for the most common type reached roughly 48,000 metric tons in 2025, while domestic sources supplied just 300 metric tons, according to Arthur D. Little data reported by Reuters. U.S. companies are on track to have capacity for about 5,000 metric tons by the end of this year, roughly a tenth of last year’s demand.

The shortage has already reached factory floors. Ford, No. 22 on the 2026 Fortune 500, idled its Chicago Explorer plant for a week in May 2025 when rare-earth supplies ran short.

China has repeatedly tightened rare-earth export controls, and American companies are still struggling to secure enough supply, Reuters reported on Nov. 6, citing U.S. officials. That day, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer pressed Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng to fully carry out Beijing’s commitments on rare earths and other critical minerals.

A major war could further disrupt overseas production and shipping routes, making it harder for the Pentagon to replenish its stockpiles.

To reduce foreign dependence, Clark said, the Pentagon is pursuing a dual approach: building raw-material reserves while funding domestic mining and processing capacity.

Tungsten, a stockpiling priority Clark identified, illustrates why building reserves takes more than government spending.

The U.S. hasn’t commercially mined tungsten since 2015, when cheaper imports undercut domestic production, according to Scientific American. Its net import reliance for the metal exceeded 50% in 2025, according to the U.S. Geological Survey.

In September, tungsten manufacturer The Elmet Group won a Defense Logistics Agency contract worth up to $2 billion to supply tungsten ores, concentrates, and sodium tungstate to the National Defense Stockpile. Only $150 million of that is guaranteed. The Pentagon also committed $450 million to Elmet, including about $150 million earmarked to restart the Springer tungsten complex in Nevada.

Yet Elmet said it won’t begin deliveries to the stockpile until additional supply becomes available. To avoid diverting existing tungsten from American manufacturers, the company plans to wait until new production comes online from investments in Nevada, Australia, and Spain.

The deal illustrates a problem FORTRESS could face across the board: signing supply contracts doesn’t immediately put minerals into government vaults.

The cost of FORTRESS also remains uncertain. A senior defense official told reporters on Oct. 20 that officials are still assessing costs, Air & Space Forces Magazine reported. The magazine estimated the broader initiative could cost “scores of billions of dollars, if not hundreds of billions.”

To help cover the bill, the Pentagon is exploring public-private partnerships, Dale Marks, assistant secretary of defense for energy, installations, and environment, told Breaking Defense.

Even with capital flowing, analysts warn that supply chain independence can’t be rushed.

“The Pentagon is unlikely to meet the goals in the near term,” Clark said, pointing to the structural difficulty of rapidly stockpiling both finished weapons and raw minerals.

Chris Berry, a minerals industry analyst, told Reuters it will “take many more years” to build the infrastructure needed to compete with China’s dominant supply chain.

The first test comes soon. Starting Jan. 1, 2027, federal rules require defense contractors to stop buying rare earths, magnets, and tungsten from China, Russia, Iran, and North Korea. Washington has repeatedly granted waivers because domestic supply falls short, Reuters reported in July.

For the Pentagon, building the physical reserves may prove far harder than announcing them.

If I Could Give My Younger Self 1 Piece of Investing Advice, This Is What It Would Be

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There's no substitute for experience, but it still helps to know some important rules of thumb.

If I could give my younger self one piece of investing advice, I'd find most of the material I'd need by reviewing my first-ever investment. My first stock was Lucent Technologies, which in 1996 was spun off from AT&T, and then bought by me at age 10 with all $500 of the birthday money I had saved up from prior years. Shortly after my purchase in late 1999, it began a long decline that saw its price fall 99.2% by September 2002.

The sting of that collapse kept me away from investing for about 15 years. So here's the investing advice that I would give to my younger self if I had to do things over again -- it's one central theme, and it has three parts.

This advice seems obvious now, but new investors still need to learn it.

Funneling all your capital into one stock means one failure can wipe out most of your portfolio. Per research conducted at Arizona State University, about 4.3% of the nearly 26,000 U.S. stocks included in the analysis accounted for nearly all of the market's wealth creation above Treasury bills from 1926 to 2016. That's an argument for owning a large and diversified basket of stocks via an index fund like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.30%).

The lesson extends to the types of assets you invest in, too. The traditional 60/40 stocks/bonds portfolio split is timeless because holding bonds can make up for underperformance that stocks experience under certain conditions, and vice versa. Today, adding some cryptocurrency into your portfolio is another way to diversify even beyond that.

If you invest in a lot of different companies, some of those investments will not play out.

According to a 2014 J.P. Morgan study, 40% of Russell 3000 index stocks from 1980 to 2014 ended up experiencing a catastrophic loss, which in this case is defined as a 70% or larger drop from the peak with little recovery afterward. In the case of my first investment, in the telecom industry, the catastrophic loss rate was 51%.

The bigger point here is psychological in nature. Expecting every one of your stock picks to work out is not reasonable, but investing requires us to be rationally hopeful each time. The truth is that failure in investing is inevitable, and vastly more common than success. Quitting after the first big failure, as I did, cost me years of compounding, and not having the proper context to process the defeat in a constructive way was especially deleterious.

So when an investment does poorly, diagnose whether the cause was bad luck, incomplete or poor analysis, or something else, and then fix that and move on to the next opportunity. And then if a different investment fails, repeat the process, again and again, until your results improve. Reading about past stock market crashes can also show you how much pain is normal.

I originally picked Lucent for my first investment because it was among the first companies I knew of in general -- at age 10, I wasn't a sophisticated investor, to say the least! Its original identity before the spinoff from AT&T was the legendary Bell Labs, one of the greatest research and development (R&D) organizations of all time, which even as a child I knew enough about to admire.

Still, I did not really have a firm understanding of why Lucent's stock was going to be worth more (or, in this case, less) in the future. I didn't really have an investment thesis. And while it's certainly a tall order to develop that understanding, especially as a new investor, it's the core of the art of investing in stocks, so my advice to my younger self would be to start grappling with these issues to the extent that it would be possible to do so at that point.

Before buying a stock, I'd tell my younger self to write two sentences: one on what the company sells, to whom, and why they buy it, and one on why its profits should be bigger in five years.

Even at age 10, I think I could've worked out those questions. And while that probably still wouldn't have actually saved me from investing in Lucent, I'm sure that I'd approach the questions with a bit more sophistication in the next investment. And improving your process over the long run is the heart of the battle.

Alex Carchidi has positions in Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
